Two villages built a bridge from opposite banks and met nine metres apart. Each swears the other sighted wrong, and Illuminara has ten days to find out what both of them measured.Dve vasi sta z nasprotnih bregov gradili most in se zgreÅ¡ili za devet metrov. 01 Â· Nine Metres

Upper Wend, the north tip of the bridge Â· dawn, ten days before the flood

Two half-bridges stood out over the river like the hands of people who had agreed to shake and then thought better of it. Between the tips lay nine metres of brown water. A string stretched from rail to rail would also have shown the north span pointing nine centimetres left of the south, and that was the part that made the villages shout.

Illuminara walked out along Odo's side over decking so new it still smelled of larch. Odo of Upper Wend followed with a notebook held against his chest, the way a man holds a shield he expects to be asked to put down.

OdoI set my line from the Tithe Stone. The same stone Hanne Sull used. I closed it to a centimetre, twice. You are going to tell me she sighted wrong. I will carry you over the river on my back if you can show it.

IlluminaraI am not going to tell you that yet. I want to know what each of you would have to be wrong about, and I want to see it myself. Ten days to the flood. After it the ferry will not run until April. Upper Wend has eleven women due before then, and one midwife, who lives on the other bank.

OdoSeren Ashby. I have known Seren since she was nine. Do you think I need reminding?

IlluminaraNo. I think you needed someone to say it, so you would not have to say it first.

He looked at her, then at the water, the way you look at a debt.

OdoIf you say we were both right, you are saying the river lied. Rivers do not lie. Somebody sighted wrong, and it was not us.

She did not argue. Behind the anger stood a man who had spent two summers lifting beams to a line he trusted with his name, and she would not take it from him before there was something to put in its place.

IlluminaraThen show me how you sighted. Slowly, as though I were new. I will do exactly what you say.

Nobody had asked Odo to teach anything since the closing day. He set down the notebook, took the sighting vane out of his coat, and began.

02 Â· Two True Lines

Lower Wend, the south landing and the survey shed Â· midday

The ferry was a flat raft on a rope, poled by its keeper, Pell Garrow, with the patience of a man paid by the crossing. Halfway over, the rope stopped taking the weight and a length of old anchor chain took over, rattling through the sheave. Illuminara watched it pass through the block and noticed that she had noticed.

Seren Ashby was waiting on the landing with a leather bag at her feet, sixty years old and solid as a gatepost.

SerenEleven in Upper Wend before March, and I have caught nine of the last ten. If you are here to be moved by my courage, save yourself the trouble. I would rather walk.

IlluminaraThen I will try to get you something to walk on.

Hanne Sull ran her survey the way she ran her tea, strong and measured to the gram. Each page of her survey book began with the weather drawn small in ink: a sun, a cloud, one grim little rain. She taught Illuminara the chain, the plumb, the vane and the arithmetic of closing a line, and watched with folded arms while the visitor did everything wrong and then right.

By evening Illuminara had re-run both crews' work with the crews' own hands. The north line closed to eight millimetres, the south to six. She laid the two books side by side on the shed table and could find no error in either larger than a thumbnail.

HanneI told Odo that in July. He said it was impossible, which is what people say when it is not impossible.

IlluminaraIt is not. Both lines are good, so the lines are not what moved. You each began from one place. What was it?

HanneThe Tithe Stone, on the north terrace. On rock. It cannot have moved.

She stopped, pen lifted.

HanneLast April my foresight to the south pier came out nine centimetres off the stone's line. I wrote it down as chain stretch. Chains stretch.

IlluminaraThe stone is on rock, so it did not walk. What stands on something that can?

HanneThe meadows. The flats between the terrace and the water. Nobody lives on them. We only cross them.

IlluminaraIs there anything down there that belongs to neither of you?

HanneThe tithe-stones. One every hundred paces down each lane to the ford, set before either village kept a warden, and written in the parish book. The bridge is laid on the ford's line.

IlluminaraThen the stones are a tape nobody here owns.

Alone in the dusk, Illuminara laid her palm on the south footing and then, crossing, on the north. Both gave the same message, the way a rope tells you someone is pulling the other end: two great blocks either side of nothing, each drawn gently away from the other. A feeling was a hypothesis, and nobody was going to hang a bridge from her palm.

03 Â· The Sum

The ferry landing, Lower Wend Â· evening, the fourth day

The test was cheap and could embarrass her. If the ground between the rock and the river had stretched, the old stones would say so. They stood a hundred paces apart down each lane, they were written in the parish book, and they belonged to neither crew. She had asked each builder to re-chain the other's bank stone by stone, set the length against the book, and bring back one number.

Odo had gone south at dawn and been escorted through Lower Wend by a dog. Hanne had gone north at noon, set foot in Upper Wend for the first time since the closing day, and asked a child to show her the lane. Now they stood at opposite ends of Pell's raft, the only neutral ground in the valley.

OdoThree point six. From the south terrace to the water, her lane is longer than the book says by that much. Eleven cracks and steps hold some of it: the orchard wall, the lane by the well, the churchyard step. The rest is in the clay.

HanneFive point four, in yours, the same way. A door in your low lane sticks so badly the baker climbs through the window.

OdoHe enjoys it.

Nobody said the sum. It lay on the planks between them like a fish.

PellNine.

He had been leaning on the pole with the face of a man deciding whether to confess to a small crime.

PellI have pulled this raft on a hundred and eighteen metres of cable for nineteen years. At harvest it parted at the south ring. I spliced it, and the ends would not meet, so I shackled on nine metres of my father's anchor chain and told myself I had measured wrong. A ferryman who miscounts does not advertise it.

IlluminaraTaut or slack, when you measured? Spliced, or sagged?

PellTaut as a harp. Three times, mostly hoping to prove myself wrong.

Illuminara waited. Two measurements, made by people who disliked one another, against stones neither of them owned, had landed on one number, and a third, made by a man who had hoped to be wrong, had landed on it too. Had she been mistaken, the stones would have agreed with the book and the cable would have fitted.

OdoSo the river did not lie.

HanneThe valley. It opened at the harvest, while we were stacking sheaves. Each of us built to a true line, and the ground walked off from under it.

IlluminaraThrough the meadows, not through the villages.

HanneThe houses stand on rock. Nobody noticed, because nobody lives in a meadow. The bridge was the only thing we had built on one.

OdoI called you a liar in front of both villages.

HanneI called you a worse word. I will think about taking yours back after the bridge stands.

It was not forgiveness and Illuminara did not reach for it. It was the first sentence either of them had said to the other that included the word after.

04 Â· Three Designs, Refused

The north abutment shed Â· evening, day six, rain on the tarpaulin

They sat at one table, which had taken six days and a great deal of tea. Odo drew on the back of a seed catalogue, Hanne in the margin of her survey book, and Pell held the lamp and a mug of something he called medicinal.

OdoWe lengthen my span by nine metres. A new pier, a new beam. Twelve weeks.

HanneThe flood is in four days. Mine is the barges: moor the Marl and the Teasel nose to tail across the gap and call it a pontoon.

OdoThe first crest takes it. And the ice takes what the crest leaves.

HanneAnd twelve weeks takes Seren's winter, Odo.

They both turned, with something like relief, to the visitor who could have ended the argument in an afternoon.

IlluminaraI can swing a twelve-metre beam across the gap, set it on both tips and be gone by dinner. I am saying it aloud so that you can say no.

HanneNo.

OdoNo. Then it is yours, and when it cracks in March you will be somewhere three skies away, and who do we shout at?

HanneA bridge that nobody here built is a bridge nobody here can mend.

Illuminara wrote not mine on the catalogue and underlined it. She felt, absurdly, as if she had been given something.

IlluminaraYou are both designing for the valley you surveyed. Design for the one that exists. A bridge that expects to be pulled, and is not frightened when it is.

PellThen a floated span? You bring the beam in on the barges at a rising river, and you do not lift it. You settle it.

It took the evening to turn a ferryman's guess into a design. Hanne knew barges and mill sluices, where a timber rides in a slot so a frame can move without cracking. Odo knew what a bearing could stand. Hanne drew the slot; Odo made it forty centimetres at each end, because the valley had opened nine metres in a night and nine centimetres since April, and he would not be the man who built a bridge that assumed it had stopped.

HanneThe slot closes by a hand's width, we meet. Both crews. Nobody waits for the village council.

OdoAnd the stop. If the staff on Pell's landing reads one point nine, the span does not go. Either of us rings the bell twice, and the barges are cut loose.

IlluminaraThen that bell is yours, not mine. If it rings twice I go home. And this is all I can do, so plan around it: I can hold the two barges against the current for forty minutes. Not the beam. The lines carry the beam. I take the shocks. After that I am no more use than a sack of grain.

HanneForty minutes is enough if we do not argue.

OdoWe will not argue.

Pell, who had known them both for nineteen years, raised his medicinal mug and said nothing at all.

05 Â· The Lowering

The gap, midstream Â· afternoon, day nine

The river was rising at six centimetres an hour and the staff on the landing read one point six two. Sixteen people on each bank held the lines, two sets of them crossed, bow to opposite stern, so that neither barge could walk ahead of the other. The Marl and the Teasel lay side by side in the gap, nose upstream, with a hand's breadth of brown water between them, and the box beam rode across both on its cradles: twelve metres of larch and iron that two villages had built in three days, a metre and a half of it over each tip, trembling in the current like a horse that has seen a ghost.

Illuminara went into the water at the bows. It was colder than she had decided to expect. She laid a palm on each barge and felt the river lean on them, a crowd with a single bad idea, and she leaned back.

HanneValves shut, tanks dry. Freeboard?

OdoForty centimetres on the Marl, with the beam aboard.

HanneForty on the Teasel. That floats the beam twice over, so one sticking valve will not sink us. Level to the hand.

OdoCross-lines taut. Pins ready.

PellOne point six three.

It was what six an hour promised. At the tenth minute an alder came down the middle of the river with its root-plate up, turning slowly, and every head on both banks turned with it.

OdoOne bell.

He rang it once. Everyone froze. On the south bank Hanne's crew swung out a boom pole and walked the trunk off the Teasel's quarter with a long, rude, patient shove, and the bell was silent.

HanneClear.

PellOne point six six.

He said it twice, because the first time nobody wanted it.

PellThree centimetres since the tree, and four minutes of them. That is not six an hour. That is forty-five. The water is brown with rain I cannot see.

Hanne did the sum aloud, because someone had to, and Odo did it after her to see that it matched.

HanneValves at twenty-four minutes, beam seated by thirty-three, pins by thirty-five, lines off at thirty-seven. The staff will read one point eight three.

OdoSeven under the line. Nine minutes of river we do not own.

HanneThen nobody opens a valve late.

IlluminaraI have forty minutes. You are asking me for thirty-seven.

HanneI am asking you for thirty-seven.

Nobody rang. Illuminara counted minutes in the weight on her hands. Sixteen. Eighteen. At twenty, the river tried something different, a sidelong shove from the south, and she took it in the arms and the spine and gave nothing back.

PellOne point seven four.

HanneValves. Open, together, on my count. Three. Two. One.

The barges sank, very slowly, under the weight of the water they had been told to take, and the cross-lines creaked and held, and the beam went down with them with a sound like a very large animal breathing out. It took nine minutes, and nobody afterwards called them short. The north bearing met the north tip. The south bearing met the south. The slot took the first of its forty centimetres of travel with a small, clean tick.

OdoNorth home. I have a hand under the shoe: seated.

HanneSouth home. Daylight under both cradles.

OdoPin.

HannePinned.

PellOne point eight two.

HanneCross-lines off. Marl first, then the Teasel. Slack, not pull. There is nothing holding that beam up now but the stone.

At thirty-seven minutes the lines came off. The Marl came out clean from under the span, stern first, hauled by the north crew. The Teasel, pumped light as she cleared, slewed, found the current and went away downriver, riding high and empty, and Hanne watched her go and said nothing for a long time.

OdoI will build you another.

HanneYou will build me a better one. Come to my yard on Thursday.

Illuminara climbed the bank on shaking legs and sat in the mud. At dusk Seren Ashby came to the north tip with her leather bag. She waited until Odo and Hanne had walked it twice, then went out onto the span, stopped where the beam met the tip, and set one boot across the dark seam. The plank moved a hair under her. She nodded to it as one might to a horse. Then she walked into Upper Wend, and no one said anything, because nothing larger could be said.

06 Â· The Slot

The bridge, midspan Â· five weeks later, hard frost

The crest had come at two point six on the staff and gone, and the bridge had stood it with water slapping the soffit and every pin in its place. Seren had crossed six times since. The Wend ferry, retired to the bank, had a small boy asleep in it.

A week ago a tremor, smaller than the one at harvest, had walked through the valley. Pell had come out on the bridge with a bucket of hot water for his hands and a tape and found the slot a little narrower. Now, on the first morning with ice on the rails, the two crews stood at midspan with their hands in their sleeves, and Illuminara stood slightly to one side, having been asked to come and not asked to do anything.

OdoThirty-seven.

HanneThirty-seven. It started at forty. Three centimetres, in a week.

OdoThen the valley is not done.

HanneNo. And the bridge is not, either. When it reads ten, we come.

Illuminara laid her palm on the beam, out of habit. Hanne watched her do it.

HanneWe have a tape.

IlluminaraI know. I wanted to feel it.

HanneFeel it afterwards.

The book on the rail was the bridge-warden's book, which had two hands in it already, Odo's square and Hanne's flat, and a third in the margin, Pell's, who had been named warden by both villages and been paid by both, to his visible alarm. Odo read the figure aloud, Hanne wrote it, and each of them signed beneath it, once.

SerenMarta's boy came on Tuesday. I walked.

She went by them with her bag, with the plank ringing very faintly under her step. They were not friends. They were two people who had agreed to read the same tape, and over a gap that had once been nine metres, that was enough to walk on.

What the critic still says doesn't work

Illuminara discovers that Odo and Hanne measured from the same fixed truth while the ground itself moved, then helps them test and freely build a maintainable moving bridge whose safety belongs to both crews; Seren crosses before the flood, the span survives later movement and shared stewardship remains after Illuminara leaves.

Tonight's shape

Six scenes across ten days and one frosty morning: a builder's accusation at the north tip, a ferry crossing and two survey books that agree with themselves, the old stones and the cable that add up to nine, a table where three designs are refused, a lowering against a river that rises faster than forecast, and a joint that is read aloud every winter.

PublishedObjavljeno 2026-10-23T19:15:00Z Â· Permanent linkTrajna povezava

01 Â· The Seam

The plains of Orrel Â· morning, nineteen days before the Concord's predicted seal

Dray Vennick had kept the fault station for thirty-one years, and in that time the seam over the plain had done nothing but breathe: a shimmer like heat above a road, only cold, in and out once every fourteen hours. This morning it had company. Something stood in the grass on the far side of it, patient as weather, and the air around it sounded like a choir tuning up.

He had made tea for the wrong number of people. The Concord's chair arrived before sunrise in a grey coat, with two assistants she told to wait by the car. Illuminara came a quarter of an hour later, on foot, because she wanted to see the plain before she saw the problem.

Maru TessalonYou are not here to open it.

IlluminaraI'm here to listen. I can't do anything useful until I know what I'm listening to.

The answer came from the grass, from the sky, and from a point just behind her left shoulder, each part finishing the sentence the last one had begun.

Ilvane-in-manyWe are four thousand two hundred. We asked before we came, and we want that known. We have not touched your side of the seam. We will not, unless we are let.

TessalonThe Concord exists to prevent exactly this. Not you. This. A mind that arrives and changes the numbers the world is built from, because it is kind and frightened and has nowhere else to go.

She said it flatly, like someone reading out a rule she had once cried over. Illuminara did not argue. She sat down in the wet grass, a body-length from the seam, and Dray, who had never seen a person do that on purpose, brought her a cup.

02 Â· An Ordinary Afternoon

The plains of Orrel Â· the same day, noon

She asked them what was ending, and then she did the difficult part, which was to stop talking.

Ilvane-in-manyThe ordinary kind of future. Things still happen with us. They no longer lead anywhere. Tomorrow arrives as a copy of today, a little thinner each time, like a page photographed from a photograph.

IlluminaraShow me one.

Ilvane-in-manyA woman puts a kettle on. She wonders, for the length of a breath, whether to make tea or the other thing. We have had her afternoon for four hundred of our years. It is always tea. The wondering is the last live thing in it, and we can feel it going.

Illuminara asked for the whole afternoon, and they gave it to her. It took four hours. Dray carried out a second chair and then a third. Tessalon stood with her arms folded until the light changed, and at some point stopped standing.

Somewhere past the second hour Illuminara noticed that she was holding her breath each time the woman reached for the kettle. She was waiting for her to choose. When the hand came down on the tea again, she felt the small shut click of it somewhere under her ribs, and understood that this, over and over, was what they were fleeing: not death, which they could have borne, but the end of being able to want the other thing.

IlluminaraThank you. I'd like you to know that someone heard the whole of it.

Ilvane-in-manyNobody has heard the whole of it. We did not know that we wanted that.

03 Â· The Third Group

The fault station, back room Â· evening

Tessalon would not be comforted, and Illuminara had the sense not to try. She told it standing at the window, with the seam breathing behind her in the glass.

TessalonForty years ago, at Veth, a smaller fault opened and one ark came through. Dense, gentle, begging. The Concord of that day said yes, because they could not bear to say no. Everything they carried arrived at once, and the numbers slipped by a part in a million. Nobody died. The bread at Veth Quay has not risen properly since. Glass clouds. Every year someone's child asks why the harbour looks tired.

IlluminaraI'm sorry. Truly.

TessalonDon't. Answer me. Every desperate delegation is sincere. Your sympathy is exactly what the next one will use. Tell me what you will do when the third group isn't so polite.

It was a good question, and Illuminara found she did not have the answer. She said so. Then, because it was true and she was tired, she said the other thing as well.

IlluminaraI could hold the seam wide and take the shift into myself. I think I could. I think it would hold.

TessalonAnd then you would be the keeper of a universe nobody asked you to keep. It cannot consent, Illuminara. That is the whole of my office: to stand in for something that cannot say yes. You offer to carry what is not yours to carry, and you call it kindness.

There was no cruelty in it. Illuminara looked at her own offered hands, and put them away.

IlluminaraThen that's off the table. What I'd like instead is to find out what the number actually depends on.

04 Â· Thirty-One Years of Weather

The fault station, pantry Â· night

The Concord's instruments recorded peaks, because peaks were what the Concord feared. What Dray recorded was everything. In the pantry, between the flour and the kettle descaler, were thirty-one years of school exercise books in which he had written, twice a day, how the seam looked and which way the wind had gone.

DrayI never thought anyone would ask. It breathes. Fourteen hours in, fourteen out, and after it has been pushed on, it settles back. I wrote the settling down because my father's logbook did.

Illuminara read until her eyes went dry. Tessalon came in at midnight to tell her to stop, saw the columns, and read as well. By the third bell of the night they had it on the back of a seed catalogue.

TessalonIt digests. The shell takes in a fixed amount per breath of the seam and gives back what it can. Spread across the breath, it leaves no mark at all. Veth was not too many. Veth was too fast.

IlluminaraThe number depends on how quickly they arrive, not how many. They could come in as spread out as you like.

TessalonThey won't. They're a single body. They came here dense, and that is how they'll cross, in one stride, to our shame and theirs.

DrayThere's one odd page, if you want it. Eleven years back a freight shimmer from the far side leaned on the seam the whole of an afternoon. I'd have said it would tear. It went thin as a thread and would not pass a thing. I wrote sulking.

TessalonNoise. One afternoon, one shimmer.

DrayI wrote it down anyway.

TessalonThe tables say the seam seals in nineteen days.

DrayThe tables are peaks. These are days. Mine say it has been narrowing since spring. A narrowing thing isn't a closing door. It's a throat. It will be one by the nineteenth day, and it will stay one.

TessalonThen we have a wide lip for nineteen days and a narrow one for good. And the guests think they can only cross in one stride.

Illuminara went back out into the dark and asked the one question nobody had asked them.

IlluminaraWhy one stride? Why all together?

Ilvane-in-manyBecause the last one to cross crosses alone. We could come as small as you like. We could come a mind at a time over a thousand years. But whoever waits, waits in the dark with the seam closing, and nobody in the last years would see the faces of the people who had already gone. We would rather arrive together than be counted out one at a time.

They were not asking to be guests. They were asking to be a gradient, and had never been told that this was something one could ask for.

05 Â· The Quiet Room

The plains of Orrel Â· dawn, sixteen days before the throat

She said the limits first, because she had learned that a gift without its limits is a trap.

IlluminaraI can make a room. I can hold it against the inside lip of the seam so that it belongs to neither of us, and I can keep it for the sixteen days the lip stays wide, until the seam has narrowed enough to look after itself. Four hundred of you, no more, because that is as many as the lip can take at a safe pace in that time. It will cost me the use of my hands for those sixteen days, and I will not be able to leave. I will not carry any one of you into Orrel. Not one. That belongs to the Concord, a person at a time, and the Concord can say no.

Tessalon had not expected to be named in a promise. She sat down across from the grass.

Ilvane-in-manyWhy would you do that?

IlluminaraSo that nobody waits alone. So that the people who decide about you have to be in a room with you while they do it. I don't have a better reason. I would like there to be one.

A small voice detached itself from the chord, as if one string had decided to be heard on its own. It said its name was Sef. It was, as far as anyone could tell, the youngest of them.

SefI want to go first. If someone has to find out what the other side is like, let it be the one with the least to lose.

IlluminaraThat's not a thing to say lightly.

SefI'm not saying it lightly. I'm saying it small. It is the size I come in.

Tessalon looked at the youngest voice for a long moment. Then she took out her notebook, and began, a little to her own surprise, to write the terms.

06 Â· The Stone

The fault station, instrument bench Â· twelve days before the throat

They tried it first with a stone. The guests parted with a pebble of their unliving substance, no heavier than a walnut, and sent it through as dense as the whole of them would have been, to see how the world would take a lie told in miniature. The third needle on Tessalon's dial quivered by a hair, and exactly as far as Dray's old columns had said it would.

TessalonAgain. This time as fifteen, spread across one breath of the seam, evenly, a pebble about every hour.

Fifteen pebbles passed in the long breath, and the needle sat as still as a drawing of a needle. Tessalon painted a hair of red on the dial, at the mark the shift must never cross, and wrote the rule on the station door in chalk where anyone on the plain could read it.

TessalonIf the needle touches the red, the crossing stops. No appeal, no speech. Fifteen to a breath, spread across it, never more. The key to the lever is Dray's. A second key is held by whoever Orrel's farms elect. Mine does not open it. I will not be the only hand on the door.

DrayI have never voted for anyone I was not related to.

TessalonThen you will find that quite a lot of the plain is related to you.

TessalonOnce more, and ugly this time. All fifteen in one instant, as hard as you can push.

The guests pushed. Four pebbles crossed before the lip caught. The needle crept to a hair short of the red and went back, and the other eleven slid away on the guest side as if a hand had flattened them.

DraySulking.

TessalonNoise.

She heard it as she said it, and said nothing more for a while.

IlluminaraA group that isn't polite gets the first stumble and then a wall.

TessalonFour in fifteen, from a pebble, in one gulp. Spread across the breath, none. I can write a rule around a first stumble. I could never have written one around no wall at all. The red stays. A pebble is not a people.

A woman in a harbour coat had come over the plain on a cart and stood reading the chalk with her lips moving. She gave her name as Ansa Mereth, of the Veth Quay delegation, which nobody had thought to invite.

Ansa Mereth'No one is pushed on.' We were pushed on.

TessalonYes.

Ansa MerethWe will vote against your Charter.

TessalonI would think less of you if you didn't. Dray's columns are open to you, and the needle logs from every test, today's included, and from every crossing to come. Take them to your harbour and test it for the slip. If the fault was ours, I will say so on your quay, in front of the bakers.

Ansa MerethThe bread has been wrong for forty years.

TessalonThen let us find out why.

Ansa took the logs. She did not say thank you, and Tessalon did not seem to have expected it.

The guests asked what would happen to someone halfway through when the lever fell. Tessalon told them honestly: they would be held in the Quiet Room and neither pushed on nor sent back. Ilvane-in-many took the answer in silence, and at last said they would cross under that rule, and that the rule should include a door for them as well: any one of them might withdraw, at any breath, without a reason being asked.

IlluminaraThat should be in the chalk.

TessalonIt already is. I wrote it while you were talking.

07 Â· Tea, or the Other Thing

The plains of Orrel Â· the last day, dusk

The lip narrowed all that fortnight, as Dray's columns had said it would and Tessalon's tables had said it wouldn't. Four hundred crossed in the Quiet Room's keeping, fifteen to a breath, spread across it, each one standing at the lip of the world while a Concord clerk read the host's laws aloud and the traveller answered yes, and each name was written in Dray's book beside the weather. Dray called the needle at every breath, and every breath it stayed well short of the red. By the last day Illuminara had not used her hands for sixteen days, and had learned a great deal about the patience of ordinary people.

On the nineteenth day the seam did not seal. It finished narrowing into a throat, a hair-fine door whose width the passing itself kept open and whose width the shell, breathing, steadily took back, and Illuminara felt the room lose its work as the throat took it up. It would pass about one mind a month. Dray set the live curve from the slate against his oldest column, and the two lay one on the other like a hand on a hand. He laughed out loud, startled, in the dusk.

DrayIt's a gate that won't be forced. I've been writing down a gate for thirty years.

TessalonAnd the rude ones will find it just as the pebbles did. First stumble, then wall.

Tessalon signed the Gradient Charter under protest, which she had said she would do and did, and at the last clause stopped and added one of her own. The Concord's veto at every stage would be shared with Orrel's farmers, and her own would not outrank theirs. She did not look at anyone as she wrote it.

The other thirty-eight hundred would come through the throat over three centuries, at their own pace and by their own choice. Many would wait asleep together as one chord, so that nobody waited alone. The oldest of them, which had not spoken in all those days, asked only that its name be written in Dray's book before the weather, and it was. Most of their universe was already gone and would stay gone.

Ansa Mereth stood at the back with the needle logs rolled under her arm. Her delegation had voted against the Charter, and said so, and been heard. She had not changed her vote. She nodded once to Tessalon, who nodded back, and neither of them looked away.

Sef went first through the throat, at dusk, with one hand held out in front of it as if the air might be a table. It stopped on the host side and stood very still.

SefIt's loud. Everything is loud. Is that what you meant by wind?

IlluminaraThat's what I meant by wind.

Dray was waiting with two cups. He had made them in the dark, by feel, with the dignity of a man who had waited thirty-one years for company.

DrayTea, or the other thing?

Sef did not answer for a long time. Illuminara, with her hands at last her own again, watched it take every moment of it, and let it.

What the critic still says doesn't work

Illuminara listens until the visitors' fear of leaving anyone alone becomes the real problem, withdraws her offer to decide for a universe that cannot consent, and spends sixteen days holding a neutral room while refugees, guardians, farmers and Veth's unpersuaded victims freely shape a slow, tested crossing that remains open to scrutiny.

Tonight's shape

Seven scenes across nineteen days on the plains of Orrel: the seam, an ordinary afternoon, a refusal, a pantry of logbooks, an offered room, a stone and a red hair of paint, and the first crossing.

Ten old speakers of a river language will not let it be recorded, because Aru can be spoken only between two people who owe each other something; five weeks before the grant ends, Illuminara accepts a spoon of jam, tests her theory of the grammar against its strongest rival, and finds out what a debt needs in order to be real.Deset starih govorcev reÄnega jezika ne dovoli njegovega snemanja, ker je aru mogoÄe govoriti le med dvema Älovekoma, ki drug drugemu nekaj dolgujeta; pet tednov pred iztekom nepovratnih sredstev Illuminara sprejme Å¾lico marmelade, svojo teorijo slovnice preizkusi proti njenemu najmoÄnejÅ¡emu tekmecu in odkrije, kaj dolg potrebuje, da je resniÄen.

01 Â· The Form for Rain

Harrow Landing school, recording room Â· the first day, 09:10

The recording room smelled of warm dust and new foam, and Sefa Dray played the old woman's voice before Illuminara had her coat off. It was a good voice, dry and patient, eleven syllables to a breath, and it said something about a kettle. Illuminara had spent most of her working life standing between two people who shared no word, and she knew the sound of a sentence that was not being said to anybody. This was one. Every verb in it ended on the same soft closing vowel, as if the old woman were reading out the weather.

Sefa DrayOrrel Tane, five years ago. Forty hours, and it is the last clean set we have. Since then they sit with us and talk, but not into the microphone. I have ten speakers, an average age of eighty-one, and a grant that ends in six weeks.

IlluminaraWhy does every verb end the same way?

Sefa DrayDefault ending, the linguist said. Aru has three. That is the plain one.

A hand reached past Illuminara's shoulder and pressed the pad on the machine. The kettle went silent in the middle of its boiling. A short, heavy woman in a green cardigan stood there with a jar held against her chest like an infant.

Ilu MarwenThat is the ending we use for rain, and for the dead. Orrel Tane is not dead. She sat in that chair for forty hours and spoke to a box as if the box were a funeral.

Sefa DrayShe agreed to it, Ilu.

Ilu MarwenShe agreed to a grant. Ask her again, in Aru, and see what verb she answers with.

Sefa Dray opened her mouth, shut it, and looked at the floor, which Illuminara counted in her favour.

IlluminaraYou are Ilu Marwen. You have refused the board four times.

Ilu MarwenFive, counting this one. And you are the interpreter, the one who is to make the machine work, which means you will want to learn us so that we can be kept.

IlluminaraI was asked to make the recording work. I would like to know what it is for before I decide whether it can.

Ilu MarwenYou want to learn it so it can be kept. We want it learned so that someone can be asked for something in it. Those are not the same task, and the board has only ever paid for the first.

Sefa DrayBecause the first is the only one that outlasts you. When the last of you goes, a kettle in a file is what is left.

Ilu turned to her without any heat at all.

Ilu MarwenYes. That is a good fear. I would have it too, in your chair.

Three endings, Illuminara thought. One for rain and the dead. What are the other two for?

IlluminaraTell me what the other two are.

Ilu MarwenI will, if you ask properly.

IlluminaraHow do I ask properly?

Ilu unscrewed the jar, took a spoon from her cardigan pocket as if she had been carrying it for years against exactly this afternoon, and held out a heap of dark jam that smelled of smoke and berries.

02 Â· A Spoon of Jam

Ilu Marwen's kitchen, Harrow Landing Â· the same afternoon, 15:30

The kitchen had one window, and the river ran past it, brown and low. By the door stood a blue enamel jar, empty to the bottom, and a bucket with a rope handle. Illuminara ate the jam and it was the best thing she had tasted on Teshun, and she said so, in the common speech, and Ilu shook her head as though a plate had been dropped.

Ilu MarwenIn Aru you cannot take a thing without the verb saying who owes. You may not say it was good and leave it there. Say it again, and mean the weight.

IlluminaraThen teach me the verb.

Ilu MarwenTevma. I ask, and I owe the asking. It ends in the first of the two endings, the one that points at the speaker. Say it.

Illuminara said it. Her tongue had no trouble. Ilu listened with her head to one side, the way a woman listens to a pan she suspects of being cracked.

Ilu MarwenIt is a word. It has no weight. You said it as if nobody was owed.

IlluminaraI can learn every word the board recorded by tonight, Ilu. I can say it all perfectly. I do not think that is what you mean.

Ilu MarwenNo. You could say the whole forty hours and not once be speaking. Perfect Aru with nobody in it is the rain ending in a good coat.

Illuminara put the spoon down. She had been given things all her life, power and insight and the long attention of a great many people, and she could not remember the last time she had been handed something and been unable to give it back in the same minute.

IlluminaraWhat do I owe you?

Ilu MarwenThe jar by the door is empty. The well is at the bottom of the lane, four hundred paces. Fill the jar by hand.

By hand. Illuminara looked at the bucket, and then, for the length of one breath, at the well she could not see, and Ilu saw her do it.

Ilu MarwenI have watched you look at pipes. Do not make the water come. I would know the difference by the taste.

So she walked the lane with a bucket. The rope bit her palm on the second trip, and by the fourth she had forgotten to be clever; the word went with her, tevma, tevma, in time with the water slopping on her boots, and somewhere around the sixth bucket it stopped being a word and became the weight in her arm. On the eleventh trip she emptied the bucket into the blue jar and the jar was full, and her shoulders ached in a plain animal way she found she was glad of.

Ilu MarwenNow ask me.

IlluminaraTevma. I ask what the other two endings are for, and I owe the asking.

Ilu closed her eyes for a moment, and then answered in a voice that had changed shape, lower and rounder, a voice that had stepped across a threshold.

Ilu MarwenTevsa. You asked, and I owe you the answer. The second ending points at the other person. Ma, I owe you. Sa, you are owed. And the third is the one the board has. O. Nobody owes anybody. Rain, and the dead.

The river went on past the window. Illuminara found she was smiling like a person who has been let into a house.

IlluminaraThen the grammar is a ledger.

Ilu MarwenIt is a ledger that can talk. That is the only reason it has lasted ten thousand years on a river that floods.

03 Â· Forty Cents a Word

Harrow Landing school, side corridor Â· the third day, 18:40

Teo Marwen was nineteen and waited at the end of the corridor with a satchel and the look of a person with an appointment he did not want. He was Ilu's great-nephew, and he had been the board's best reader for two years.

Teo MarwenYou are the one Aunt Ilu fed. Everyone in the lane knows. She has not fed anyone since my grandmother died.

IlluminaraI carried eleven buckets for a spoon of jam.

Teo MarwenThen you already do it better than I do. I get forty cents a word. I read the lists into the machine, six hours a day. I am very good. The linguist says I have the cleanest vowels on the river.

IlluminaraAnd when you speak Aru to your aunt?

He did not answer for long enough that the corridor lights buzzed and clicked off on their timer. Neither of them moved to wake them.

Teo MarwenShe answers me with the rain ending. Every time. I thought I was bad at it. I thought there was a part of the language that you had to be born into and I had missed it, and that I was the one who had taken the money for something I could not even do.

Illuminara could see a shape then, the way a floor plan comes clear when someone opens the last door. It did not take the languages of forty courts to see it. It took a nineteen-year-old with clean vowels and a pay slip.

IlluminaraTeo. You are not bad at it. You are excellent. Here is what I think, and I can be wrong about it: the board pays you for every word, so every word you say is already settled. Nobody owes anybody, and the verb has nowhere to put the weight. Your aunt may not be rejecting your Aru. She may be answering the only grammar you have given her to answer.

Teo MarwenThe money cancels it?

IlluminaraIf I am right, every debt, before it is made. I would not bet your aunt's kitchen on it yet.

He sat down on the floor with the satchel in his lap. It was a long time before he spoke, and when he did, it was in the common speech, very low.

Teo MarwenBashe Corrin will not speak to me at all since I started. She is ninety-one. She taught my mother. She turned her chair to the wall when I came in last spring.

IlluminaraShe may not think you are bad at it. She may think you have been bought.

Teo MarwenWhat do I owe her, then?

IlluminaraThat is for her to say, and for you to ask. I only know that it cannot be money, and that it must be something she may refuse.

She did not tell him that the thing in his satchel, the forty hours of his own flawless reading, had frightened her more than the empty river had. If she was right, the unowed Aru was complete. A thousand patient hours could keep it exactly as it was, a language laid out like a body for the funeral.

04 Â· The Arithmetic

Board office, Harrow Landing Â· the ninth day, 10:00

Sefa Dray had made tea in two mugs and put the sugar between them like a treaty. Her desk calendar had five weeks left on it, and the last week was marked in red by someone whose handwriting looked like her own.

Sefa DrayI will say it plainly, so that you cannot say I hid it. Hale Orne has a heart that the clinic gives four months. Bashe Corrin is ninety-one. In five weeks one of them may be a name on a stone, and if the board has not recorded what she carries, nothing of it exists. I am not afraid of kitchens. I am afraid of a grammar lesson going down a hole with her.

IlluminaraYou are right to be afraid of it.

Sefa DrayThen why am I being asked to bet the last ten voices on a custom about jam?

It was the strongest thing anyone had said to her on Teshun, and Illuminara let it stand a moment before she answered.

IlluminaraYou are not being asked to bet them. You are being asked not to spend them. The recording as it stands is fluent and dead, and a hundred hours more only makes a longer corpse. What I can offer is smaller and less safe: young people who owe old people something real, and a language that has somewhere to live.

Sefa DrayAnd who pays them?

IlluminaraNobody pays them. Pay the learner and the debt is cancelled. But some things must not depend on a young person's debt at all. Nursing. Winter fuel. The clinic run. Those the board pays for, to the elders directly, whether any apprentice stays or goes, so that no child is ever the only thing between an old woman and the cold. What the apprentices owe is what only a person can owe: time, errands, being there, the carrying.

Sefa looked at the calendar. She was not stupid, and she was not unkind. She was doing the arithmetic she was paid to do.

Sefa DrayNow let me be unkind, since you are being kind to me. Anselm Voy wrote the grammar, twenty years on this river. Voy never heard debt in those endings. Voy heard the room. Rain and the dead are said out of doors to the whole lane; ma and sa at a table with the door shut. Who is listening, how formal the place is. Teo's trouble is that a microphone is a room with no one in it. Pay him or do not, it changes nothing, and you have built my winter on jam.

IlluminaraIt is a good account. It fits everything I have heard as well as mine does.

Sefa DrayThen you do not know.

IlluminaraI do not know. And you have just told me how to find out. Voy and I expect different things. If it is the room, then a formal, public, witnessed exchange will bring out ma and sa even when nothing is owed. If it is the owing, a plain private favour will, and the public, paid one will not. The paid one must be finished before a word is said: the thing handed over, the money in the hand, nothing left to return and nothing left to do. The favour must leave exactly one duty, taken freely. The same two speakers, the same new sentence, one that is not in the forty hours. Ilu and Hale Orne listen from the next room and say which ending they hear. Not me. I have told Ilu what I think; Hale has not heard it, and she speaks first.

Sefa Dray found an envelope in her drawer, wrote on a slip of paper, and sealed it with the flat of her thumb. Illuminara wrote hers on the back of a bus ticket and handed it over to be kept.

Sefa DrayIf it falls Voy's way, there is no programme. I record, and you go home.

IlluminaraYes.

Sefa DrayAnd if it falls yours, I still need something that can fail in public. I will not fund a feeling.

IlluminaraThen name the test.

Sefa DrayBy first frost, in five weeks. At least six elder and apprentice pairs, speaking Aru in the owed endings, witnessed by someone who is not you. If it is fewer, the money returns to recording and I do not apologise for it.

IlluminaraThat is fair. They may also refuse me entirely. I have not been accepted yet.

Sefa DrayI know. I asked Ilu this morning whether you were any good, and she said you had eleven buckets of water in you and had not spilled a drop on her floor.

05 Â· Behind the Blanket

Hale Orne's front room, Harrow Landing Â· the tenth day, 17:00

Hale Orne lay on three pillows with her feet against a hot-water bottle and the face of a woman who had made her peace with dying and not with being bored. A grey blanket hung across the kitchen doorway on a length of clothesline. Ilu sat beside the bed with her knitting in her lap, not knitting. Illuminara had the floor by the door. On the sideboard lay a sealed envelope and a bus ticket, and from behind the blanket came the sounds of two people trying not to scrape their chairs.

Hale OrneFour months, the clinic says. I will give you this afternoon. Do not make me hear anything twice.

Ilu MarwenYou will hear what you hear.

Sefa had tossed a coin in the yard to decide the order, and had told no one how it fell.

A bell rang in the kitchen. Someone read out terms in the flat voice of a person reading a receipt: a coat, sold outright to Teo Marwen, forty cents, paid and handed over; nothing kept back and nothing to bring back. A pen scratched. At the door a third person coughed, the school caretaker, who had been brought to stand witness and had been told to say nothing. Then Teo's voice came through the blanket, the clean vowels of the best reader on the river, saying a sentence that was in none of the forty hours.

You put the coat in my hands. Every verb in it closed on the same soft vowel.

Hale OrneRain.

Ilu MarwenRain. A man at a market.

The caretaker's boots went out and the door shut. The light under the blanket changed to a lamp's. Something was said too low to hear, and a long quiet followed, and then Teo again, with the same sentence in the same clean voice, and it was not the same sentence. The verb in its middle leaned toward the other person and stayed there. After it, a half-breath late, like a foot finding a stair that is not there, Sefa's voice answered.

Hale OrneMa. And she gave him the sa, though she was slow with it. He is frightened he will break it.

Ilu MarwenHe is frightened for the right reason.

The blanket was lifted aside. Teo came through with Sefa behind him, and Sefa took the envelope from the sideboard and opened it with a thumbnail, as if it might bite.

Sefa DrayI wrote: both of them ma and sa. The room does it. The bell, the receipt, the witness. I wrote Voy.

IlluminaraMine says the first would be rain and the second ma. I did not see the order. I only wrote what I would be wrong about.

Hale OrneYou wrote the failing guess down. Nobody ever does that.

Ilu MarwenVoy was not stupid, Sefa. Listen. A debt wants somebody to hear it closed. That is why we have the lane. Voy heard the closing, and the closing wants a witness, and he took it for the owing.

Sefa DrayThen the room matters.

Ilu MarwenFor the closing. Not for the owing.

IlluminaraTeo. What happened in the kitchen, the second time?

Teo MarwenShe held the coat out and said keep it till you are warm, and she looked at the window while I decided.

IlluminaraWhich meant you had to bring it back.

Teo MarwenTomorrow, washed. I said I would, and I meant it.

Hale OrneShe looked at the window. She left you the door.

Teo MarwenI could have said no.

Hale OrneThere. That is what I would have told your Voy, if he had asked an old woman instead of a book. The first coat was bought and finished, and nobody owed anybody for it. The second was lent, and you took on one duty by your own say-so. Had she stood over you and said take it, you would owe her nothing at all. A thing forced is nobody's. We have the rain ending for that too. You cannot owe what you could not refuse.

Ilu MarwenThat is the part I had not said aloud.

Illuminara found that she had stood up without meaning to. The design she had carried into Sefa's office had one more beam under it than she had known, and it was this one.

IlluminaraThen nobody can be assigned. Not by the board, not by me, not by an elder's need. Every morning is asked again, and either of them may say no.

Hale OrneThen you will lose some.

IlluminaraYes.

Hale OrneGood. That is how you will know the rest are real. Now go away, all of you. A girl is coming to read to me and I want to look as though I have been resting.

06 Â· Two Refusals

Harrow Landing school hall, a canteen table pulled to the middle Â· the twelfth day, 19:00

There were nine chairs at the table and two of them were empty, because Hale Orne was in bed and Orrel Tane had sent word that she had said all she meant to say to the board. Bashe Corrin sat at the head in a shawl with her hands folded on the table and her chair turned a quarter away from Teo, who stood by the wall with his satchel, and she had not looked at Illuminara once. Ilu sat beside her, and Sefa Dray sat at the foot with a folder she had not opened.

Illuminara laid out the design as plainly as she could, with what Hale and Ilu had heard on the tenth day: the endings came only where each party could have said no, so no one would be assigned and every morning would be asked again. Then she waited, because what came next was not hers to hurry.

Bashe CorrinMy brother owed a man in the old camp. Eleven years, for a roof. The man never said the closing word and my brother never got to say it for him. He died owing. He was owed for thirty years by a man who would not close it. A debt is also a chain, girl. You would teach children to owe us, and when we are dead who opens the door?

The room did not move. It was the real thing and everyone in it knew it. Illuminara had no clever answer and did not look for one.

IlluminaraYou are right. A debt with no end is a chain. Everything I have said is wrong if the one who owes cannot leave.

Ilu MarwenThe closing word is the creditor's. Aru has no word for the debtor to close his own.

IlluminaraThen the creditor says it first. Before the first errand, the elder says the closing word aloud, in front of a witness from the lane, and the witness keeps it. How long, what for, and the word that closes it, all spoken at the start. The learner may call the witness on any morning and ask for the closing, and it is said, and the debt is closed, whether the elder is fond of them or angry or asleep. The elder may call it too. The learner may leave in the season they choose. The elder may send them off.

Ilu MarwenA closing said before there is anything to close. That is not in the forty hours.

Bashe CorrinThen it is not a chain. A chain has no key in the debtor's pocket.

Ilu MarwenIt may be bad Aru. We shall see what the lane does with it.

Bashe CorrinWritten words are the board's. Aloud.

IlluminaraAloud. Never written.

Bashe Corrin considered this with her mouth very small. At last she unfolded one hand and laid it flat.

Bashe CorrinThat is a better rule than the one my brother had. It is not for me. I will not owe a child and I will not be owed by one. Put my name in no kitchen.

IlluminaraI will not.

Bashe CorrinIf anyone wishes to learn from me, they may sit at Ilu's table and listen. They will not ask me for anything.

Ilu did not argue. She had waited sixty years for this woman to say a thing she could not argue with. Instead she turned toward the foot of the table.

Ilu MarwenAnd your machine, Sefa Dray. You still want it.

Sefa DrayYes. With whatever any of you will give.

Ilu MarwenThen you ask. And whoever asks, owes.

Sefa DrayThe board cannot owe a person, Ilu. It can only be accountable to the funder.

IlluminaraThen it can write the debt into its own charter and read it aloud at this table. A recording given by any speaker makes the board the debtor. The archive is never sold, never used to teach anyone who has not first owed an elder, and stands only as long as the apprenticeships stay free.

Sefa looked at the folder. She picked up her pen and put it down, and did a thing Illuminara had not asked of her. She turned her chair to face the old woman.

Sefa DrayTevma. I ask for the voices, and I owe the asking.

Ilu MarwenBadly. Again.

Sefa DrayTevma.

Ilu MarwenBetter. Tevsa. You are owed the answer, and the answer is yes, from five of us. Not from Bashe. Not from Orrel. And Hale will answer for herself.

At the door, afterward, a thin hand closed on Illuminara's sleeve. Bashe Corrin had not asked anyone to help her with her stick.

Bashe CorrinYou carried Ilu's water. I counted eleven buckets from my window. Nobody does that for a tourist.

IlluminaraI was not being a tourist.

Bashe CorrinI know. I will tell you one thing, and you will not owe me for it. It is the sound a river makes under ice. It has no word in the common speech. We call it the glass-talking.

IlluminaraTevma, Bashe Corrin. I owe you that.

Bashe CorrinI did not ask you to.

IlluminaraNo. It is mine to say.

Bashe CorrinThen hear this too, and you will not owe me for it either. That boy stood against the wall for an hour tonight with his bag on his shoulder, and nobody stands an hour who is only paid. When I am in the ground, if he asks for the glass-talking, and asks as if it weighed, you may give it to him. I will not hear him say it. I am deciding it now so that nobody has to guess at my burial.

IlluminaraI will give it to him if he asks like that, and not otherwise.

Bashe CorrinNot otherwise.

07 Â· The Fifth Afternoon

Hale Orne's front room Â· the fifth week, a Thursday, 15:00

Merri had been sixteen for a month when she began reading to Hale Orne in the owed endings, mornings, before school. In the third week she told Hale that mornings would not do, and Hale said afternoons or nothing, and Merri said afternoons. In the fourth, with her examinations coming, she asked the neighbour from number nine to come and say the closing word. Hale, who was fond of her and had every reason to hold on, could have said no. She said: I would rather you stayed. Merri said: I know, I am asking. The neighbour said the word that Hale had spoken into her keeping on the first day, and the debt was closed, and the lane heard it, and the nurse came on Thursday as before, because the board paid the nurse and Merri had never been the only thing between Hale and the cold.

On the fifth Thursday Illuminara came to Hale's porch with a loaf from Ilu and did not go in, because she could hear the front door open and a chair drawn up, and the screen was thin.

Hale OrneThe term is closed, girl. I heard the word said. You do not owe me a Thursday.

MerriI know. Nobody does.

There was a pause, and a chair, and then Merri said something in Aru that made Illuminara put the loaf down on the step. It used the closing vowel of the rain ending, the one that said nobody owes, but it leaned on it the way the first ending leans, toward the speaker, so that the sentence meant at once that nothing was owed and that she had come, and that she wanted the old woman to know she knew the difference.

Hale Orne laughed until she coughed.

Hale OrneSeventy years, and nobody has said that to me. That is neither one thing nor the other. Ilu will call it vile grammar and then say it at supper.

MerriIs it allowed?

Hale OrneI have no idea. I have not been asked for a ruling. Read to me. Not the lists. The one with the dog.

Illuminara listened to the paper turn. Hale Orne died on the Sunday of that week, with the girl in the chair and the book open on the quilt, and the closing word was spoken at the bedside by the old woman herself in her last clear hour. It was not for the term, which had ended. It was the one she kept for what she had been given.

08 Â· First Frost

Ilu Marwen's kitchen Â· the sixth week, first frost, 07:20

The window had frosted at its corners and the river was black behind it. In the lane outside there were people carrying things. Illuminara counted them as she came up the path, which she had not planned to do, and stopped at seven: a boy with a basket of split kindling, a girl with an old man's shopping, two teenagers with a handcart of stove-wood, and three more she did not know. None of them was carrying a recorder.

Teo was in the kitchen already, kneeling at the stove with the ash-pan. He had been there every dawn for four weeks. He and Ilu had spoken the terms aloud on the first morning in front of the neighbour from number nine, who had stood holding her coat and cried quite openly: fourteen weeks, the stove and the water and the lane errands, and the closing word held in her keeping. In the third week he had told Ilu he could not do the lane errands and the stove both with his lessons, and Ilu had said the stove, then, and the neighbour had come again to hear the new term said and cried again, as far as anyone could tell about everything. He had a bandage on one thumb, where the poker had caught him.

Teo MarwenTevma, Aunt. I ask you for the morning words, and I owe you the asking, and I owe you for the day I let a machine hear me and not you.

Ilu stopped with the kettle in the air.

Ilu MarwenSay that last again.

Teo MarwenI owe you for the day I let a machine hear me and not you.

She put the kettle down. It was a sentence nobody in the room had ever heard, because in ten thousand years nobody on the river had needed to apologise to a machine. Teo had taken the owed ending, which was made for jam and water, and bent it, with fear and a little arrogance, to hold something the language had never carried. It did not break.

Ilu MarwenThat is not in the forty hours.

Teo MarwenI know. I made it.

Ilu MarwenTevsa. You are owed the answer. It is a good sentence. It is very bad grammar, and I will tell you why, and it will take all morning.

Illuminara stood in the doorway with the cold at her back and did not go in. It was not hers. She had said the first word in this house and it was not necessary that she be in the room for the thousandth.

A car had stopped at the gate. Sefa Dray got out of it in yesterday's clothes with the folder under her arm, and came up the path with the walk of someone who has been awake all night and decided against sitting down.

Sefa DrayI came in person, because it is not a thing to say to a machine. The panel met last night. They would approve a second year if the archive were opened to visiting scholars from January. I said no sale, no extraction, and nobody taught from it who has not first owed an elder. They said, then no equipment line. I said, then no equipment line.

IlluminaraAnd the winter.

Sefa DrayIs in the board's charter, read aloud by me in the school hall with half the lane standing round it, and the panel cannot reach it. Nursing, fuel and the clinic run are paid to the elders directly, whether any apprentice stays or goes. I read the clause to the panel as well. Aloud. They did not like it. It is in the minutes.

IlluminaraWhat did it cost?

Sefa DrayA year of the good microphones.

She did not sound sorry. She told the rest standing in the frost, while the kettle began again behind her: seven pairs by first frost, witnessed by the neighbour from number nine, who was not Illuminara. Five elders had given recordings, each after being asked by someone who then owed them a favour. Hale Orne had given hers in the third week, and struck a passage about a sister with her own hand. The archive was partial and unlovely, full of kettles and coughs and a child demanding to know what a particular noise meant, and every speaker had listened to their own and chosen what to withhold.

Sefa DrayThe grant is over this morning. What is left is whatever you all do tomorrow without my money to do it with.

Ilu MarwenThen come in and sit down. It is going to be a long morning.

09 Â· The Glass-Talking

Bashe Corrin's porch, Harrow Landing Â· late autumn, 16:10

Bashe Corrin had died on the eleventh day of the frost, between a Tuesday and a Wednesday, and the lane had said the rain ending for her. It was the only form that belonged to her now; no one could owe her, and she could owe no one. It sounded to Illuminara exactly as it had on the recording, a soft closing vowel, nothing weighing on it, but this time it was said by sixty people at once, standing in the cold, and the form that had been so wrong in a box was right and terrible in a crowd.

She had counted them without meaning to, as they stood round the grave. Of the seven pairs, Hale's had ended in the way pairs end. Five of the other six were still carrying, eleven days after the grant had stopped paying for anything but the winter coal and the nurse, and two of those five had spoken new terms aloud on the lane that week without anyone asking them to. The sixth had had its closing said in good order the week before the frost, and its learner stood at the back of the crowd with a basket, carrying nothing that was owed.

After the burial the porch was empty except for the chair, which had stayed turned a quarter toward the wall. Illuminara came to it alone.

She had been carrying it for six weeks: a word without terms. She had said tevma to a woman who had said she did not ask, and the debt had gone out into the world without a creditor to close it, and now the creditor had gone where none of the verbs could follow.

Ilu was already there, with the blue enamel jar under her arm.

IlluminaraI owe Bashe Corrin a word, and she is dead, and I do not know what the grammar does with that.

Ilu MarwenIt does something. It would not be the grammar if it did not.

IlluminaraYou have never told me the fourth ending.

Ilu MarwenThere is not one. There is a way of closing, and it is old, and it is rarely needed in a village where people outlive their debts. A debt to the dead is closed when you are asked for the same thing by someone living, and you give it. And there is whatever Merri said to Hale Orne on her last Thursday. Ask me again in a year.

Illuminara sat down on the porch step, which was cold, and was glad of it.

IlluminaraNobody has asked me for the sound of a river under ice.

Ilu MarwenNot yet.

Footsteps on the gravel. Teo came up the path in his work coat with kindling dust on his sleeves, and stopped when he saw the chair. He was not her apprentice. He had never owed her a thing, and had never been owed by her, and it showed in how carefully he stood.

Teo MarwenAunt told me about the word. The glass-talking. She said I might ask, if I wanted to. I have no right. Bashe Corrin never said a thing to me after the day I was paid.

IlluminaraYou do not have to ask.

Teo MarwenNo. That is why I am asking.

He did not use the short form. He said it all, slowly, in the owed ending, as if he were setting down a heavy tray.

Teo MarwenTevma. I ask you for the word Bashe Corrin gave you, and I owe you the asking, and when I have it I owe it to her.

Illuminara found that her throat did not work for the space of a few breaths. Then it did. She had meant to ask him one thing first, and she saw that he had already answered it.

IlluminaraShe left you a condition, Teo. She said it at her own table, with the lane listening, so that nobody would have to guess. If you asked, and asked as if it weighed, I was to give it to you. She said you stood against a wall for an hour with your bag on your shoulder. She said nobody does that who is only paid.

Teo MarwenShe said that. About me.

IlluminaraTevsa. You are owed the answer. It is the glass-talking, Teo. It is the sound a river makes under ice, and it has no word in any speech but hers.

She told him how Bashe had said it, with her tongue pressed at the back of the teeth and the long soft fall at the end. He tried it and got it wrong, and tried it again, and this time his clean vowels did something Illuminara had not heard in two months of Aru: they cracked, very slightly, in the right place.

Ilu had not said a word. She stood with her hand on the back of the old woman's turned chair and looked at the river, which had not yet frozen and would not for a week.

Ilu MarwenThe jar will be empty by morning.

Illuminara looked at her. Ilu's face was steady, the face of a woman who had nothing left to teach and one more thing to ask.

Ilu MarwenTevma. Tomorrow at dawn, before the frost is off the lane. Two buckets, not eleven. I am old, and I do not require much. But I would like them carried by hand.

IlluminaraTevsa. Tomorrow, before the frost is off.

She walked back down the lane with the last of the light on the frozen puddles, and the glass-talking went with her, in Teo's cracked vowels, behind her on the path.

What the critic still says doesn't work

Illuminara learns that Aru distinguishes a finished exchange from a freely accepted obligation, then helps elders, apprentices and Sefa build a speaker-controlled practice with bilateral exit, independently paid care and safeguards that survive the grant.

Tonight's shape

Nine scenes across eight weeks: a recording that speaks only to the dead, a spoon of jam and eleven buckets of water, a young man who is paid to be a parrot, a board officer's honest arithmetic and her rival grammar, a coat sold once and lent once behind a blanket, a kitchen table with two refusals, a girl who leaves and comes back, a first frost with a price paid for it, and a word that cannot be repaid to the one who gave it.
