01 Â· The Empty Place on the Banner

Renn Square Â· three hours before the opening procession

Twelve ribbons crossed the square from roof to roof, each woven in a different district pattern. Beneath them, the translation lattice hung like a second, invisible roof: microphones in the cornices, light-letters along the stage, and a calm synthetic voice prepared to turn every sentence into flawless Standard.

The largest banner named the young performers who would open the Festival of Returning Voices and Sena Marru-kei, the honoured elder whose opening call would answer them. Eleven names shone in gold. The twelfth line read SENA â€” UNSUPPORTED NAME ELEMENT.

Lio Marr stood on a ladder with a pot of river-blue paint. He had painted his grandmotherâ€™s missing clan-name over the warning three times. Each time the latticeâ€™s maintenance light erased it.

LioIf it deletes her once more, I will paint it across the directorâ€™s coat.

Siva Renn did not look up from the safety console. She wore the black sash of the old-language survivors and kept one thumb pressed against the brass stop key.

SivaThen the coat will be removed from the programme as an unverified surface.

LioThat was almost a joke.

SivaIt was a warning with good timing.

Illuminara landed outside the ring of rehearsal lamps, softly enough that none of the paper boats arranged around the fountain stirred. She had come to help the festival open without renewing the violence that had closed the square twelve years earlier.

She read the banner, then looked at Lio rather than at the machine.

IlluminaraWhat should that line say?

Lio descended. He touched the blank strip with two paint-stained fingers.

LioSena Marru-kei. Marru-kei is not decoration. It means she belongs to the people who return a borrowed boat with fish in it. It is also the sound her mother used when she called her home.

SivaIt has fourteen disputed glosses and one obscene resemblance in South-bank speech.

LioMy grandmother is eighty-one. She is not a disputed gloss.

At the foot of the stage, Mara Venn folded a survivorâ€™s chair and carried it away. A white scar crossed her jaw where a bottle had broken during the riot.

MaraNeither was my sister. A word that sounds harmless in one mouth can arrive armed in another.

The square quieted around her. Lio set down his brush.

IlluminaraAre you leaving because his name is dangerous, or because the lattice removed it?

MaraI am leaving because everyone here has decided I must choose between those two dangers.

Siva finally looked up. The console showed a field of green confirmations: twelve years without a language-triggered assault inside the civic zone.

SivaThe lattice gives every person the same safe sentence. Nobody has to guess what was meant.

LioNobody gets to mean anything the machine cannot flatten.

Behind him, the youth troupe removed their throat-microphones one by one. No chant accompanied the refusal. The little clicks of metal on wood were enough.

SivaIf you speak without mediation, the opening licence is void.

LioThen there is no opening.

Illuminara watched Sivaâ€™s hand tighten on the stop key. This was not a censor enjoying obedience. It was a person holding a door shut because she had once seen a crowd come through it carrying fire.

IlluminaraShow me the sentence that made you build this.

Sivaâ€™s expression closed.

SivaThere was no sentence. There was a funeral.

IlluminaraThen show me the funeral accurately.

Mara stopped beside the empty chairs. After a long moment, she set one back down.

MaraI will stay for that. Not for a lesson. For the accurate part.

02 Â· The Translation That Could Not Be Answered

Civic archive beneath Renn Square Â· two hours and seventeen minutes before opening

The archive kept the riot in three rooms that disagreed with one another. One held damaged recordings. One held official transcripts. The last held objects people had carried home: a scorched drum, a childâ€™s red shoe, a sign split through the word RIVER.

Siva unlocked the recording room but remained standing in the doorway.

SivaMy brother Jaro was nineteen. He heard a North-bank drummer issue a challenge. He answered it. By sunset seven people were dead.

LioThat is the school version.

SivaIt is also the version in which I washed his blood out of my sleeves.

Lio looked down. His anger did not vanish, but it lost the easy shape it had worn in the square.

LioI am sorry about Jaro.

SivaDo not use him as the price of your courage.

Illuminara asked for the original recording rather than the certified transcript. The archive clerk had marked it unintelligible because three speakers overlapped and the drum rhythm interrupted the consonants.

She listened once at human speed. Then she listened again, separating the air vibrations without changing them, letting each voice sound through a different wall panel.

A womanâ€™s voice said a phrase in North-bank speech. The official transcript rendered it TAKE THEIR RIVERBANK BEFORE DUSK.

The drum beneath the phrase used the flood-warning rhythm, not the challenge rhythm.

IlluminaraWhat else can the verb mean?

LioHold. Keep. Brace with your body.

MaraOr refuse to yield.

SivaAnd in Jaroâ€™s district, take from someone.

Illuminara moved to the next recording. A North-bank elder tried to interrupt the civic relay fourteen seconds after the first translation. Her microphone had been closed because the official channel permitted one certified rendering.

At thirty-one seconds, another translator submitted HOLD THE FLOOD BANK TOGETHER. The relay labelled it contradictory and suppressed it.

At forty-eight seconds, the first bottle broke.

Siva replayed the sequence. Then again. On the third pass she sat beside the scorched drum.

SivaYou are saying the riot was a software error.

IlluminaraNo. People threw bottles, chose targets and killed. The error did not remove their responsibility. But the city gave one frightened translation the authority of fact and denied every living speaker the right to answer it.

MaraWe were told certainty would keep panic down.

LioCertainty locked the wrong door.

Siva touched the split sign, tracing the burnt edge with one fingertip.

SivaFor twelve years nobody has died here because of a word.

IlluminaraFor twelve years nobody has been allowed to discover whether they can repair one.

Siva rose so quickly that the chair struck the wall.

SivaYou will leave after the applause. I will be here when a joke becomes a funeral again.

The words landed without theatrical force. They were the exact weight she had been carrying.

Illuminara did not answer at once. She looked at Jaroâ€™s name in the official transcript. The last vowel had been regularised into Standard.

IlluminaraHow did he say his name?

SivaThat is not relevant to the control failure.

IlluminaraIt is relevant to you.

Sivaâ€™s first attempt was barely audible. The name rose at the end, then folded inward on the final breath. The transcript had made it brisk and square.

Illuminara repeated what she heard and got the fold wrong.

SivaNo. You are making it sound like a door closing.

IlluminaraAgain, please.

Siva said it again. Illuminara listened again. The third attempt was still imperfect, but Siva did not stop her.

MaraWhat changed?

IlluminaraI thought we needed a translation system that made fewer mistakes. We need a public practice that prevents any mistake from becoming unanswerable.

SivaA practice is not a barrier.

IlluminaraThen do not trust the idea. Design a test harsh enough that you could trust the result.

03 Â· Say It Wrong Where We Can Hear

Renn Square rehearsal ring Â· ninety-four minutes before opening

They returned to find the audience gates already crowded. Rumour had travelled faster than the festival notices: the youth troupe had refused the lattice, and the woman from the sky intended to abolish safety.

Illuminara asked the technicians to leave the lattice powered but remove its authority. Original speech would sound first. The display would show named human glosses side by side, with uncertainty visible instead of silently resolved.

Siva did not surrender the stop key.

SivaAny survivor or speaker can call a pause. Ninety seconds. No argument during the first thirty.

MaraAnd the person whose words are disputed speaks first after the pause.

LioNot the loudest translator.

SivaNot the director.

Lio painted three amber circles on the stage floor. One for the speaker, one for the person harmed or alarmed, one for a chosen glosser. Nobody would be forced into a circle; an empty place would remain visible.

Illuminara stepped to the microphone with a list of twelve district and clan names. She could have analysed every mouth in the square and reproduced the sounds mechanically. Instead she used only what each person had freely taught her.

IlluminaraI will say these as I currently hear them. If I am wrong, correct me. The correction will not be an interruption. It will be the work.

The first four names passed with small adjustments. On the fifth, she placed the stress too early. Laughter moved through the North-bank seats.

Illuminara heard delight before she understood the joke. It was bright, shared and entirely unavailable to flawless Standard.

LioYou just called the Lantern Boat clan the clan of damp trousers.

IlluminaraHow fortunate that I wore neither.

The laughter widened, including two people in survivor sashes. Siva did not laugh, but her hand eased around the key.

Then Illuminara reached Sena Marru-kei.

She spoke the first part correctly and let the final syllable fall. A man near the eastern gate stood so abruptly that his chair overturned.

Man in the east seatsThat ending is what they called us before the fires.

Several young performers answered at once. Their voices collided. The lattice flashed SAFE STANDARD AVAILABLE.

Siva struck the brass key.

Amber light filled the square. The microphones muted for thirty seconds, including Illuminaraâ€™s. She did not override them.

A barrier at the eastern aisle buckled as frightened people pressed backward. Illuminara crossed the distance in a blur, braced the rail with both hands and opened a second path without lifting anyone or deciding where they should go.

Mara reached the overturned chair. She righted it and faced the man.

MaraDo you want the middle circle, or do you want me to carry your words there?

Man in the east seatsCarry them. I do not want all those eyes.

When the first thirty seconds ended, Mara entered the circle alone and repeated his objection. Lio waited in the speakerâ€™s circle until she finished.

LioMy grandmotherâ€™s clan-name is Marru-kei. The insult uses the same last sound, but it cuts the first vowel short. I should have told the stage team before asking everyone to know the difference.

MaraCan the difference be shown before it is heard?

LioA blue river mark. Our speakers already use it in letters.

Siva turned to the man. He remained beside his seat.

SivaWould the mark and the speaker-first rule be enough for rehearsal to continue?

Man in the east seatsFor rehearsal. Not a promise about tomorrow.

SivaThat is all I asked.

The man studied the black survivorâ€™s sash at Sivaâ€™s shoulder.

Man in the east seatsI wore east-bank grey at the funerals after yours. We learned whose black sash kept the key. What was your brotherâ€™s name?

SivaJaro.

He repeated it once in Standard. Siva corrected the rising vowel and the inward fold, not gently but exactly. He listened, then shook his head when Mara offered him a phrase card.

Man in the east seatsNot yet.

The answer was not forgiveness. It was consent for the next bounded step.

Siva released the key. The blue river mark appeared beside Marru-kei, but the lattice also displayed the eastern listenerâ€™s warning and the two different vowel lengths.

IlluminaraSena Marru-kei.

This time Lioâ€™s grandmother called from the front row.

SenaCloser. You still return the boat with only one fish.

The square laughed again, and the man in the east seats sat down instead of leaving.

04 Â· The Ninety-Second Proof

Renn Square Â· forty-eight minutes before opening

Siva wrote the proof conditions on the main display herself. The rehearsal would use three phrases selected by survivors, three selected by performers and one selected without warning by a mixed audience panel. Any participant could call the ninety-second pause. The speaker could correct every display, and the correction would remain in the public record beside the earlier error.

SivaA machine failure does not void the proof if we catch it, honour the pause and keep the correction beside the error. One uncaught or concealed substitution, and we use Standard tonight.

LioOne ignored pause and we walk.

MaraOne person mocked for correcting us and I close the survivor section.

IlluminaraAnd if all seven phrases are repaired without threat or concealment?

Siva looked toward the archive stairs.

SivaThen I sign a licence for one night. Nothing more.

The first two phrases passed with both glosses visible, one South-bank blessing drawing startled laughter.

The third phrase used a kinship form Standard translated as dependent. Sena corrected it to the person whose bowl remains at my table even when absent.

At the fifth phrase, the lattice failed its two-second threshold. The screen displayed a confident translation while the human glossers were still divided.

Sivaâ€™s hand moved toward the stop key before anyone else called the pause.

SivaHold.

She looked at the confident line still shining above Maraâ€™s divided glossers. For one breath the square was twelve years younger: one frightened rendering wearing the authority of fact.

SivaThe lattice has failed. The proof now is whether we catch the failure without hiding it or punishing the answer.

Amber light returned. This time no one surged. People put down their programmes and waited through the first silent thirty seconds.

The disputed phrase came from Mara. In her district it meant I remember what was taken. In Lioâ€™s it sounded like I reserve the right to take it back.

MaraBoth meanings live in it. I chose the first. I cannot make the second unreal.

LioThen my gloss should say what I feared, not what you secretly meant.

MaraYes. And mine should say what I intended, not what you were required to hear.

The two sentences appeared side by side. The lattice marked neither as final.

Siva stared at them. Twelve years earlier, the city had hidden precisely this kind of disagreement in order to prevent panic.

SivaWhat stops a liar from using uncertainty as a shield?

IlluminaraNothing automatic. The original recording remains. The affected personâ€™s account remains. Consequences remain. Uncertainty prevents the translator from becoming a witness it was not.

MaraAnd the pause gives us time to ask who benefits from the ambiguity.

LioA safeguard is not innocence.

Siva let the sentence sit. Then she added a rule: deliberate misuse would be reviewed by a rotating panel that could suspend a performer, but never erase the original speech or the objections attached to it.

Before the seventh phrase, the mixed panel offered the eastern man the blank card again. He could have passed it to anyone. After watching Maraâ€™s correction remain beside the machineâ€™s error, he wrote one word and folded the card shut.

The seventh phrase arrived unannounced to Siva from the eastern man. It was Jaroâ€™s name in her home dialect.

The square became utterly still.

The lattice offered the Standard form. Lioâ€™s glosser panel left the line blank and turned toward Siva.

LioSpeaker first.

Siva did not move for several breaths. No rule required her to speak. The empty amber circle remained open.

Then she stepped into it.

SivaHis name rose here.

She touched her throat.

SivaAnd folded here, as if the sound were making room for the person who answered.

She said Jaroâ€™s name. Lio repeated it and missed the fold.

SivaNo. Not a door closing.

LioAgain, please.

She said it again. He listened again. On the third attempt, the shape held.

The eastern man called no pause. Mara did not ask Siva to forgive the city or the crowd. Sena simply moved a survivorâ€™s chair closer to the stage.

Siva signed the one-night licence.

SivaKeep the lattice awake. Let it show how often it does not know.

05 Â· A Boat Returned with Fish

Renn Square Â· midnight

The Festival of Returning Voices opened forty-one minutes late.

Every performance took longer than the programme allowed. Original speech came first. Two or three human glosses followed when they differed. Pale bars showed uncertainty. Blue river marks warned where a cherished name touched an old insult. Amber circles remained empty until someone freely entered them.

The youth troupe began with a comedy in three dialects about a ferryman who accepted payment in prophecies but could not make change.

Half the audience laughed before the first gloss. The other half laughed when the second gloss revealed that change also meant a dry pair of trousers.

Illuminara stood behind the eastern aisle, not on the stage. She repaired a cracked rail, carried water to a woman whose hands shook after a pause and listened as twelve districts corrected her festival greeting.

Three survivors chose to leave before the comedy. Their empty chairs were not removed or filled. Mara attached small cards to them: LEFT BY CHOICE. RETURN WELCOME. NO EXPLANATION OWED.

One performer was suspended after repeating an ambiguity for a cheap laugh when he had been warned it named a massacre. His original words, the objection and the panelâ€™s decision remained visible in the record.

The festival did not prove that every living voice was safe. It proved that safety no longer required pretending only one voice was real.

Near midnight, Lio rolled out the restored opening banner. The twelfth line read SENA MARRU-KEI, with the blue river mark and a note inviting the bearerâ€™s own account.

Sena examined it sternly.

SenaThe fish is too small.

LioIt is a symbol, Grandmother.

SenaThen symbolise generosity.

He painted a larger fish. The audience applauded the correction, not the painter.

Siva watched from the safety console. The green field that once counted years without reported language incidents now showed tonightâ€™s totals: eleven pauses, eleven resumptions, twenty-eight public corrections, no hidden substitutions and no assaults.

MaraWill you keep it?

SivaThe one-night licence expires at dawn.

Mara waited.

SivaAt dawn I will propose thirty nights. Rotating panels. Survivor veto over the pause rules, not over other peopleâ€™s names. Speaker ownership of corrections. Independent audit of every confident machine gloss.

Mara unfolded one of the spare chairs and placed it beside the console rather than behind it.

MaraIf the survivor section chooses me, I take the first dawn rotation.

SivaYou said you would not choose between two dangers.

MaraI will not. I can help hold the pause without becoming its only keeper. So can you.

Siva looked from Maraâ€™s chair to the eleven recorded resumptions. The brass key no longer sat beneath one hand.

IlluminaraI cannot promise to be here when the next joke turns dangerous.

SivaNo. But the record will be, and Mara will not be alone at this console. Leave us something we can practise without you.

IlluminaraThen I will leave the listening equipment, and you will keep the right to change the practice.

MaraAnd if the council refuses?

SivaThen I will show them tonightâ€™s record and ask them which risk they are pretending does not exist.

She removed the black survivorâ€™s sash, folded it carefully and placed it beside the brass stop key. She did not discard either.

Lio approached with a wet brush and enough restraint not to threaten her coat.

LioThere is space on the banner for Jaro, if you want it.

SivaNot as a reason everyone must agree with me.

LioAs a person whose name should not be made square.

Siva took the brush. She painted the rising mark first, then the inward fold. Her hand shook on the final stroke, and nobody reached over to steady it without being asked.

When she stepped back, Illuminara said the name once.

Siva listened.

SivaCloser.

IlluminaraOnly closer?

SivaCloser is how a living language says continue.

Above them, the twelve ribbons pulled differently in the river wind. Their knots held because none had been forced to become the same ribbon.

What the critic still says doesn't work

In a city where a flawless translation system once let one frightened rendering pass for fact, Illuminara listens to a recording until she hears a flood warning under the word the transcript called a threat, then helps Siva and the festival replace the machine's final word with a public pause, side-by-side glosses and speaker-owned corrections, so a banner name is restored and nobody is forced to forgive.

Tonight's shape

Five scenes. A civic opening collapses when the safety lattice deletes a living clan-name; an archive reveals that the old riot was hardened by one uncontestable translation; an honest public mispronunciation becomes a repair instead of a spark; a timed rehearsal tests community safeguards under pressure; and the festival reopens with slower speech, visible uncertainty, voluntary pause rights and names restored.

Now a second storm is thirty hours out, sixty-one boats are still beyond the strait, and the woman whose husband was on the lost boat decides whose voice the fleet will hear.Pred tremi dnevi je IlluminariÄina napoved nevihte varno pripeljala domov dvesto ribiÅ¡kih Äolnov, enega izmed njih pa poslala na sipino, ki je ni kazal noben zemljevid. Zdaj je druga nevihta oddaljena trideset ur, enainÅ¡estdeset Äolnov je Å¡e vedno onkraj oÅ¾ine, Å¾enska, katere moÅ¾ je bil na izgubljenem Äolnu, pa odloÄa, Äigav glas bo posluÅ¡ala flota.

01 Â· The Pier

Little Skerry, harbour pier Â· thirty hours before the second storm, 18:05

The fleet had been in for three days and had not gone back out. Two hundred boats lay nose to tail along the breakwater with their nets wet and their masts still, and the only light on the pier burned over the fish-scale hut where Marit Okonjo stood with her back to the water, as if the sea were someone she had not yet decided to speak to.

Illuminara walked the length of the pier without hurrying. She had rehearsed nothing, on purpose. She stopped two paces short and began before Marit could turn.

IlluminaraMy track for the first storm was eleven kilometres east of where the storm went. That put five point one metres of swell on the Gannet Run at the hour Hesper Dawn crossed it, not four point three. I called the Run safe. I called it safe because the chart gave nine metres of water on the Run, and because I never asked anyone who lives here whether the chart was true.

Marit turned. She was fifty, small, with a face the weather had been editing for decades.

Marit OkonjoYou've had three days to practise that.

IlluminaraThree days to check it. It comes out the same each time.

Marit OkonjoInes Varga has a daughter at the university in the city. Dov Hallam is nineteen and bought a guitar he can't play. Lise Harrow can't swim well. My husband holds the end of the net because he says it's the place where you can see everybody's face. Say their names. I want to hear if you've got them right.

IlluminaraInes Varga, skipper. Dov Hallam. Lise Harrow. Tobiah Okonjo. I don't know which of them is alive. I have watched the drift charts every hour since Tuesday and I don't know.

She had imagined that saying it would feel like setting something down. It felt like lifting something. She noticed that she was holding her hands very still, the way one does near a thing that might break.

Marit OkonjoNow you tell me you've fixed the model. Second storm is thirty hours off. Sixty-one boats from the outer villages are still on Gannet Roads, sixteen hours from home, and the Run is the only way in that beats the weather. You'll want my fleet on your second guess.

IlluminaraI won't tell you it's fixed. I don't know what else it can't see. I came to find out what it can't see, and to ask who is allowed to refuse it.

Marit OkonjoEveryone. Me first.

IlluminaraThen I'd like to be refused properly, by people who've looked at the numbers. Let me take them to the Pilots' Hall and let the old pilots tell me where I'm wrong.

Marit OkonjoMy husband is out there and you want me to sit in a hall.

IlluminaraI want you to find him. I can't say where he is. I can say where the water would take him, and only if the pilots tell me what this water does that my model doesn't know. Without them I'd be guessing where to look, and I've guessed enough.

Marit looked at her for a long time. Behind them a halyard rang against a mast, once, and was still.

Marit OkonjoOne hour. After that I decide who talks to my fleet, and it isn't you.

02 Â· The Hall

Pilots' Hall, Little Skerry Â· twenty-eight hours before the second storm, 19:40

The hall smelled of tar and old paper. Fourteen chairs stood in a ring, and on the knee of the oldest pilot lay a ledger bound in sailcloth, its pages ruled by hand into columns that no chart-maker had ever seen. Wenna Tolliver was seventy-one and did not get up. A girl of sixteen sat on the stairs with a hydrophone in her lap, winding its cable round her hand, pretending not to listen.

Illuminara unrolled her bathymetry on the table and weighted the corners with Wenna's own tea mugs, because nobody had told her not to.

Wenna TolliverFive-year-old chart. The Choir isn't a rock, girl. It's a bank, and a bank walks.

IlluminaraWalks how fast?

Wenna TolliverSlowly, until a gale. Then in a night. The note tells you. Water goes over the crest and sings, higher when it's shoaler, the way a whistle rises when you pinch it. We call it the Choir because it was once a hymn, and it's been out of tune with the chart since the September blows.

IlluminaraWhat note is the chart's nine metres?

Wenna TolliverA low E. I've not heard a low E on the Run in a month.

Pip MarrenIt's an A, Wenna. I took it Sunday. Nobody asked me.

Wenna TolliverThat's why you're on the stairs, Pip. Be quiet and be useful.

Illuminara did the sum where all three of them could see it. A swell breaks when it is more than three-quarters as tall as the water under it. In six metres of water, four and a half metres of swell breaks. Hesper Dawn had met five point one.

IlluminaraThen the chart was wrong and my track was wrong, and either one right would have kept her out of it. I took both because they agreed with each other.

Wenna TolliverThat's the worst reason to take anything.

IlluminaraYes.

Wenna turned a page of the sailcloth ledger without looking at it. Her thumb knew where the page was.

Wenna TolliverYou'll want the marks. Eighty years of them, a note and a depth and a date, written down in this hall and nowhere else. Give them to a table and the table gives them to a firm in the city, and the firm sells a sea to every trawler from the south. Then we're all pilots and nobody is.

IlluminaraThen they don't leave the hall. I won't copy a page. You read me the note, I give you the swell, and you decide. Nothing goes in my model that you didn't say aloud.

Wenna TolliverSlower. Good. Slow is what keeps people alive. Ines Varga trusted a lane because it was on a screen, and a lane on a screen has nobody inside it to say no.

IlluminaraI'd like that written down too.

Wenna looked at her for the first time as one looks at a stranger who might turn out to be a colleague.

Wenna TolliverTonight you come out on the Run with Pip and me. I'm not describing a note to someone who has never heard one. If you're going to be wrong again, you'll be wrong in a boat.

03 Â· The Choir

Arrow Strait, the Gannet Run Â· twenty-four hours before the second storm, 23:50

The skiff was small and smelled of diesel and cold coffee. Pip held the tiller and Wenna sat in the bow with the hydrophone's cable over her knee, and Illuminara sat amidships with a lamp that she was told not to shine on the water. The Run was only a line on the chart; in the dark it was a long loose silence between two white seams of surf.

Pip lowered the microphone. The earphones went round once and Illuminara took them.

It began as a hum, the sort that lives in a refrigerator, and then as they crossed the mark buoy it climbed, steadily, like a voice going up to ask a question. She heard it bend and thicken.

Pip MarrenThat's the A. Last month it was an F-sharp.

Wenna TolliverSix metres, six and a half. And it's coming up. Hear that? It's still walking.

IlluminaraMy model has nine here.

Wenna TolliverYour model has a chart.

Illuminara did not answer. The note held in her ears, and she found it was much harder to doubt than a number. She had never been afraid of a number, she realised. She was afraid of this.

Wenna TolliverTake us north, Pip. Three hundred.

They crept across the Run with the engine at a mutter. The hum dropped as they went, a tone, then another, then a long low note at the edge of hearing, and the earphones filled with something almost like breath.

I could have asked. Any day this year, I could have asked.

Pip MarrenLow G.

Wenna TolliverEleven, twelve metres. The crest used to sit here, before it walked south onto the Run. It left a trough behind it.

IlluminaraA lane.

Wenna TolliverA lane that's a lane tonight. I'm not telling you it'll be one tomorrow. Tomorrow you ask me again.

IlluminaraEvery time. At the outer mark, with the note, before any boat crosses.

Wenna TolliverHmph.

The skiff drifted. Far to the east the sky had a stain on it where the second storm was assembling, and nobody mentioned it.

IlluminaraWho did the Choir take?

Wenna TolliverMy brother Jory, forty-one years back, on a day so flat it looked ironed. I told him the note was a D, and it was an E-flat. I said so in the hall the next morning, to everybody, before one of them could ask. That's the only reason they let me keep the book.

She said nothing else about it. Pip, at the tiller, turned her face away and studied the dark with great seriousness.

Illuminara took the earphones off. The sea went on without her. For a moment she let herself want something very small and very selfish, which was to have been out here three days ago, in this boat, hearing this.

04 Â· The Terms

Pilots' Hall Â· twenty hours before the second storm, 04:10

Marit had not slept, and had not pretended to. The sailcloth ledger lay open on the table under the hall's one good lamp, and beside it three sheets of paper, each headed in Wenna's hand. Illuminara had brought two mugs of tea. Marit refused the first and drank the second without comment.

Marit OkonjoTell me what you found.

IlluminaraThe Run is dead. The bank is under it, six metres, shoaling. The storm's swell will break there from early evening. About three hundred metres north there is a trough, eleven to twelve metres, left behind when the crest walked. Wenna calls it the Slot. If the pilots' note holds, the fleet can use the Slot and cross ahead of the weather.

Marit OkonjoIf the note holds.

IlluminaraYes. That's the load. It's why I'm not the one to say it.

Marit OkonjoGood. Here's how it goes. Three predictions, written in the book before the sun's up. The old table says nine metres, no break. Wenna says the note's an A, a break sometime after eight. Yours says the pre-swell reaches the Run at about six-forty and the Slot stays quiet with a low G. Pip takes the skiff out at first light and writes down what she hears. If yours doesn't beat both of the others on something we can see, nobody uses it.

IlluminaraThen put a number in it that I can be caught on. Write the width of the break. If it can't be wrong in a way you can see, it isn't a forecast, it's a prophecy, and I've already given you one of those.

Marit OkonjoHow wide?

IlluminaraTwo hundred metres of white across the Run. Write it last, under the others, in my name.

Marit wrote it. Her pen did not hesitate, and Illuminara understood that she had just made herself easier to refuse, and that this was what she had come to do.

Marit OkonjoAnd if yours is right?

IlluminaraThen it's still not mine to use.

Marit OkonjoNo. Wenna calls the marks on the radio. I call the fleet, in my own voice, because they'll answer mine. You stay on the pier.

IlluminaraA sixteen-year-old in a skiff, under a swell.

Marit OkonjoShe's the best skiff hand in the strait and I'm on the radio. Abort rule: if Pip hears anything higher than a B on the Slot, the water's shallower than eight metres and the fleet stays out under the lee of Gannet with sea anchors. I never call the Slot. That costs sixty-one boats a night at anchor in a bad wind. I know what it costs. I'm telling you it's mine to pay.

Illuminara did not answer at once. She had a great many ways to reach that skiff other than waiting on a pier, and the hall had written no rule against any of them. She also knew that a lane the fleet used because she had gone and made it safe would be hers, and that the whole of the night's work had been to make one that was not.

IlluminaraAgreed. All of it.

Marit OkonjoThat took you a while.

IlluminaraIt took as long as it should.

Marit turned a page of the ledger she did not need to read.

Marit OkonjoTobiah.

IlluminaraTwo places. With the pilots' eddies in the drift it tightens to Gull Tooth ledge, where Wenna says the flood sets anything that floats, or the Tail Race. Nobody lives through the Tail Race. Today's spring tide covers the ledge a little after two in the afternoon. A boat has to be there by half past eleven, by the inner passage. That one isn't the Run. It's sheltered.

Marit OkonjoDon't tell me he's alive.

IlluminaraI don't know that he is. I'll be on the pier until he's either on it or someone has told you he won't be.

Marit OkonjoI didn't ask you to stay.

IlluminaraI know. I'm telling you where I'll be.

Marit said nothing. The lamp hissed. Out in the dark somebody was already hauling a skiff down the slip. Pip, Illuminara realised, who had been awake as long as any of them and had not been asked to sleep.

05 Â· The Test

Little Skerry, harbour pier Â· seventeen hours before the second storm, 06:30

The radio sat on an upturned crate between Marit and Illuminara, and neither looked at it. Wenna's voice came out of it dry and level, calling the marks as if reading a menu. Out on the Run a small light moved against a grey sea. The pre-swell was already visible as a slow shouldering of the horizon, like something large turning over in its sleep.

Pip MarrenRun mark. I've got the A. It's a clean A. Bank's where Wenna said.

Wenna TolliverSwell?

Pip MarrenLong. Long and low and it's lifting me.

Marit OkonjoSix thirty-eight.

Marit wasn't speaking to anyone. She had the three sheets of paper in her fist.

Pip MarrenIt's coming up under me. I'm going to the Slot. Wenna, I'm going to the Slot now.

Wenna TolliverGo.

Illuminara heard the engine rise. The skiff was a mile out under a breaking sky with one girl in it, and Illuminara could have been beside it before the next sentence ended. She stood on the pier with her hands at her sides, because she had said she would, and because a lane she had to carry a girl through was not a lane. It was the hardest sum of the night, and the answer was to do nothing.

Pip MarrenThere it goes. Oh. Oh, it's breaking. It's breaking white across the whole Run. It's more than two hundred, it's, I don't know, three hundred and fifty, I can see the whole edge.

Marit OkonjoSix forty-one.

Marit did not look at Illuminara. She did not need to. A hundred and fifty metres of white where her name stood on the sheet for two hundred.

Pip MarrenI'm at the Slot mouth. It's lifting. It's a B. It's not quiet, Wenna, it's a B.

Marit OkonjoA B is not higher than a B.

Wenna TolliverIt's the mouth. The Slot starts further north than your girl thinks. Pip, forty metres. Along the white post and then forty. Listen for the G.

IlluminaraMine had the mouth too far south.

Wenna TolliverYours had it where the chart put the old crest. The crest walked sideways too. I can hear that. You couldn't.

Pip MarrenNorth forty. North, north. There. G. That's the G, Wenna, it's quiet, it's lifting but there's no break.

Nobody spoke on the pier. Marit read the three sheets again, and then, slowly, once, without hurry, she put them down on the crate.

Marit OkonjoTable said no break. Wrong. Wenna said the bank right and the hour wrong by an hour and a half, and the mouth right. Yours had the hour to the minute, and the Slot, and the width a hundred and fifty out, and the mouth forty out. Wenna's ear moved your mouth and your hour moved her break. Neither one puts a boat through alone.

IlluminaraMy bank came from Wenna.

Marit OkonjoAnd the hour came from you, off a track you got wrong and a swell period you got right. I'll use the second.

She picked up the fleet microphone, and for a moment her hand did not seem to belong to her.

Marit OkonjoGannet Roads, this is Marit Okonjo, fleet-captain, Little Skerry. Come in by the Slot, three hundred north of the Run and not by the Run, and forty past the white post. Wenna Tolliver calls the note at the outer mark. You cross on a G or lower. You hear a B, you hold, you drop the sea anchors and I'll answer for it. This is my call. Repeat it back to me.

The voices came back one by one, crackling, sixty-one of them, each repeating the same four sentences in a different accent. Illuminara counted them and found she was crying and had not noticed. She wiped her face with the back of her hand as if it were spray.

Marit OkonjoYour name is in the book.

IlluminaraWhere?

Marit OkonjoOn the left. In the column Wenna keeps for what we got wrong. She wrote it herself before you'd sat down.

06 Â· Gull Tooth

Gull Tooth ledge, outer skerries Â· thirteen hours before the second storm, 11:10

They went by the inner passage, which was sheltered and slow, in a boat that Marit helmed herself. She had said Illuminara would come, and that she would hold the lamp and touch no rope. Pip, wet through and radiant, sat in the stern with the hydrophone in her lap. Wenna's voice, from the hall, hummed in the speaker like an insect in a jar.

Gull Tooth was a black shelf the size of a kitchen, awash on every third wave, with a reef in front of it where the swell stood up and fell. There were two figures on it, lashed to a stanchion bent from some wreck, and a third shape, long and still, wrapped in a yellow oilskin.

Pip MarrenMarit. There's two of them standing.

Marit did not answer. She had a hand on the throttle and the knuckles had gone the colour of bone.

IlluminaraThe surge on the reef is running ten minutes high and then easing. The next slack is eleven minutes long and begins in ninety seconds. If the gap is where the pilots' notes put it, you have time to cross once.

Wenna TolliverThe gap's two boat-lengths south of the white post, on an E. You hear an E, you go. You hear an F, you stop and I'll curse you.

Marit OkonjoPip.

Pip MarrenE. I've got an E.

Marit OkonjoThen we go.

It took four minutes, the boat dipping and rising, the hydrophone singing its single note, Marit's wet cheek turned toward the ledge. The wave stood up behind them, hesitated, and passed. Then they were under the stanchion and the lamp lit two faces, grey and swollen, one of them laughing in the helpless way of someone who had not expected to.

Tobiah OkonjoYou took your time.

Marit OkonjoI was in a hall.

The three of them were lifted over the gunwale, Lise Harrow first, then Tobiah, and then the long yellow shape. Dov Hallam was dead, and had been since the first night, Tobiah said, which was why he had lashed him there. Marit was the only one who touched him, gently, the way she had once touched her own sleeping children. Ines Varga was not on the ledge. Tobiah said so once, quietly, at the tiller, and Marit nodded and did not make him say it again.

On the way back he looked across the boat at the stranger holding the lamp.

Tobiah OkonjoMarit says you said our names on the pier. Before anyone asked.

IlluminaraI didn't know which of you were alive.

Tobiah OkonjoThat's what she told me you said.

Illuminara held the lamp until her arm ached. She did not set it down, because nobody had told her she could.

07 Â· The Count

Little Skerry, breakwater and Pilots' Hall Â· two and a half hours before the second storm, 21:30

The rain had come early. Fifty-two hulls were in, and the breakwater lamp showed them one at a time as they slid past the pier, each skipper standing at the wheel and looking at no one. Wenna counted with a stub of pencil and her tongue between her teeth. Marit stood at the end of the breakwater with the fleet microphone in her fist. Illuminara stood two paces behind her, where she had been told, with the lamp hanging from her hand. Out at the mouth of the Slot, Pip's skiff rode the swell with its hydrophone down, and the speaker on the breakwater carried what Pip heard: one long, even G, like a held breath.

Pip MarrenFifty-two in. G. Next is Rook.

Aud HallamMarit, Rook. I've the mark in sight. Coming through.

Marit's thumb was already on the key when the note moved.

Pip MarrenB. I've got a B.

Wenna TolliverA B's allowed.

Pip MarrenIt's a B and it's walking. It's, Marit, it's a C.

The rule had been written in the book at four in the morning and had been nothing since but ink.

Marit OkonjoHold. All boats, hold. Rook, hold. Sea anchors down and keep your heads.

Aud HallamMarit. I have been sixteen hours on this water to get to my son. He's on a bench in your hall and I'm sixteen hours late. Don't make me wait on a note.

Marit OkonjoYou wait.

Aud HallamWhose number is that?

Marit OkonjoMine.

Marit lowered the microphone and, without turning her head, spoke to the person behind her.

Marit OkonjoIs it the Slot, or the surge?

IlluminaraThe surge. The pulse on the reef runs ten minutes and the last two ran ten. The note should fall in about six.

Marit OkonjoShould.

IlluminaraShould. I'd hold until Pip says G regardless.

Marit OkonjoI wasn't asking you when. I was asking what.

IlluminaraI know.

It took seven minutes. Nobody spoke on the breakwater, and the wind got into the radio and sang a note of its own.

Pip MarrenG. G, Marit. It's gone down. G.

Marit OkonjoRook first. Come through.

They came through, nine hulls in the rain, and the last of the lamps showed Rook's skipper at the wheel with her chin up. As she passed the pier her voice came out of the speaker, level, and not for the sea.

Aud HallamIs she there? The woman who did the sums.

Marit OkonjoShe's behind me.

Aud HallamThe first sum cost me my boy. The second brought me in to him. I'll carry both, and you'll not ask me to put either one down.

IlluminaraNo.

The sixty-first hull slid in past the pier at ten thirty-five. Wenna put a tick in the sailcloth ledger and sat down on the nearest bollard as if her legs had been taken from her.

Inside, the lamp was low. Tobiah lay on a bench under a blanket with a mug of broth. Lise had been taken home. Dov lay under a grey blanket at the end of the hall, and his mother sat beside him with her hand on the blanket and her back to the room.

Marit OkonjoI'm not forgiving you.

IlluminaraNo.

Marit OkonjoInes Varga's daughter lands on Thursday. You'll tell her what you told me, in this hall, with the book open, and she'll say whatever she likes. If it's nothing, it's nothing. If it's worse than I said, you'll hear it.

IlluminaraYes.

Wenna TolliverAnd when the sea drops, Pip and I take the skiff down the Tail Race eddy. A mark an hour on the ebb, fifteen of them. You give us the swell for each mark and we say which mark. If the daughter wants to ride along, she rides.

IlluminaraI'll bring the swell. You choose the mark.

Marit OkonjoAnd the next time there's a storm, you ask the hall before you tell the harbour. Not after. Before. In the book, as a rule.

IlluminaraI'll write it myself. In my own hand, on the left.

Marit almost smiled, and it went out like a match in a draught.

Tobiah OkonjoLise says the woman with the lamp never put it down, Marit.

Marit OkonjoNobody told her she could.

Tobiah OkonjoThen somebody should.

Nobody did. Illuminara found she had stopped waiting for it.

Wenna had come in from the cold and stood dripping by the stove. She laid the sailcloth ledger open on the table, three columns now where there had been two: what the table said, what the water said, what the hall did about it. Beneath the first storm the left column held a line in her old square hand. Illuminara read it, and did not look away.

Wenna TolliverCome here and hear this.

She switched on the speaker at the end of the table. Out beyond the breakwater the hydrophone still hung where Pip had left it, and through the rain it carried a long, low, steady note, a G, the sound of a lane open in the dark, with sixty-one hulls resting above it, and a hundred and twenty miles of weather coming in behind them to find nothing it could break.

What the critic still says doesn't work

Illuminara admits the fatal limits of her first forecast, learns from Wenna's tide-hearing, makes her replacement prediction refusable and fallible, and helps Marit bring sixty-one boats home without taking the harbour's knowledge or authority from its people.

Tonight's shape

Seven scenes across thirty hours: an error said aloud before anyone asks, a hall of old pilots who will not give up their marks, a night on the water learning to hear a bank, terms set by the woman Illuminara wronged, a dawn test in which the hall and the model each catch the other's miss, a rescue timed by two kinds of knowledge, and a crossing at the breakwater where the abort rule is used, a harmed voice is heard without absolution, and a count ends that is not forgiveness.

01 Â· Burns by the Wall

Sorrel Gate, upper terraces Â· 12:40

Smoke stood on the western ridge, six kilometres off and leaning toward them. The district had given Sorrel Gate until three o'clock. Illuminara had spent the morning walking the upper terraces with a tablet of creek gauges, which was what the regional water office had sent her to do, and she had stopped doing it at the first black patch.

There were forty-one of them now. Each was the size of a tablecloth. Each was still warm when she held her palm an inch above it. None lay in open grass. Every one lay on the sheltered side of a drystone wall or the lip of a terrace, and not one had crossed the stone.

Someone is counting the wind, she thought. Someone is lighting only where the wind has been refused.

Taavi Brandt came up the track with a drip torch hanging unlit from his belt and a young crew lead named Joss Marren a pace behind. He was fifty-odd and broad, with the stillness of a man who had learned to spend his words last.

Taavi BrandtSomebody has been lighting my hill since six this morning. Eleven fires I can account for. I'd put the rest on the same hand.

IlluminaraForty-one. I've walked them all.

Taavi BrandtThen you've walked forty-one places where a fire could have got away from a person. Constable Hale is at the church with an order for Ivet Corrandon. She's eighty-one, she won't open her door to my crews, and at dark I carry her down that road myself. I would sooner she walked.

IlluminaraLook at where they are, Chief. Not at who.

He looked, because he was fair and because she had said it quietly. He looked at the nearest patch and the wall above it, and for the length of a breath he said nothing.

Taavi BrandtCalder Fold. Nine years ago. I lit a backburn, lawful and signed, in a wind that behaved for four hours. In the fifth it veered and the fire took the track and then a barn, and there was a man asleep in the barn. Aldous Penn. Careful fires are the ones that kill people. The careless ones don't last long enough.

IlluminaraThen I won't argue that she's careful. I'll ask whether she's right, and look for an instrument that can answer without either of us having to believe anything. Let me speak to her first.

Taavi BrandtUntil two. At two the buses load, and she's on one or she's on my shoulder.

02 Â· What the Hill Was For

Corrandon cottage, kitchen Â· 13:15

The door was not locked. Illuminara knocked anyway, and a voice told her through the wood that it never was, that people simply believed it was, and that she could come in if she had the sense to wipe her boots.

Ivet Corrandon sat at the table with ash to the elbows, a match tin at her right hand and a blackthorn stick against her chair. Across her left palm ran a blister the width of a finger. She had the face of someone who had been right for a long time without anyone's help.

Ivet CorrandonYou're the water girl. They said a water girl was on my walls with a machine.

IlluminaraI'm a water-modeller. The machine is a tablet and mostly it counts creeks. May I sit?

Ivet CorrandonIf you've come to take me to the bus you can sit on the floor.

IlluminaraI've no authority to take you anywhere. I came to ask about the wall at the top of Pellam's field.

The old woman looked at her for some time. Illuminara took the clean cloth from the dresser while she waited and asked, with her eyes, about the hand. A question can wait and a blister cannot. Ivet let her do it, watching with open suspicion and then, slowly, with less.

Ivet CorrandonIn nineteen eighty-four a forestry man stood in this kitchen and told my Edrin the hill must not be burned, not by anyone, not ever, and he took the paper that said we could. Edrin was fined. He didn't speak for a week. Forty years we've lived under a hill with nothing to do but grow.

IlluminaraAnd you went on burning.

Ivet CorrandonSmall. At the end of the wet, on the lee of the walls. Edrin called it combing. A fire that comes over a ridge is hungry, and it eats everything between itself and the next thing to eat. Comb the lee of a wall black and the headfire reaches the stone, looks over, and finds nothing for its mouth. A wall with no black behind it is only a stack of stones.

IlluminaraThen why not tell the chief?

Ivet CorrandonHe lit a hill once and a man died. I read it in the paper. He'll hear old woman, and he'll hear liability, and he will not hear wall.

She said it without cruelty, as she might have given the weight of a loaf. Illuminara had been wrong about nothing in this kitchen so far, and she had the feeling that would not last.

Ivet CorrandonAnd you. Your machine has never smelled a slope at four o'clock. It's never stood where the air changes its mind.

IlluminaraNo, it hasn't. So tell me where your map is wrong. I would rather find that here than on the ridge.

Ivet CorrandonNowhere.

The silence went on long enough for the kettle to tick. Ivet looked at the dressed hand, and at the stick, and at the window.

Ivet CorrandonThe north. The gully above the Marrick cottages. I ran out of knee. Edrin never combed the gully. He said it burns itself. Then he never said another word about it, and I never asked.

Something turned over in Illuminara, quietly, like a key in a lock she had been leaning on for three weeks. The gully was the old stone leat, the flood channel she had modelled for the water office. Every storm she had run went down that cut like thread into a needle. Water finds it. So does anything else that wants to go downhill.

IlluminaraMrs Corrandon, would you tell me the order you burn in? Not the reasons. The order. I'll carry it to the chief as something he can test, with his own torch, on his own say.

Ivet CorrandonAnd if his test says I'm a fool?

IlluminaraThen a fool's mistake is corrected in thirty metres, instead of a village's in three hundred. Either way the hill is the winner.

03 Â· A Test He Could Stop

Sorrel Gate school, command post Â· 14:05

Two buses idled in the car park with their doors open. Brandt stood over a trestle table with the district map pinned under four stones, and he did not look up until she had laid her tablet on top of his county. Over his shoulder, Joss Marren was loading a hose reel and trying not to listen.

IlluminaraShe isn't lighting fires. She's combing the lee of every wall so a headfire has nothing to eat behind the stone. It's a firebreak drawn in fire. Forty years old, and the state forbade it for all of them.

Taavi BrandtThat's what she says.

IlluminaraIt's what the walls say. I read them as load paths, and a load path is an interpretation, and she could be right by accident. So I'm not asking you to believe her. I'm asking for one segment. Thirty metres of the east wall, your crew, your torch, your hose behind it. If the burn does not die at the lee by itself, you put it out and we've lost twenty minutes.

Taavi BrandtAnd if it dies by itself, I've learned about one wall. The headfire isn't one wall.

IlluminaraNo. But it's a way of turning a coincidence into a measurement. And I'd ask you to hold the second thing against it as well, because I think she's right about the walls and I'm afraid of the place she hasn't burned.

He took a long time. A bus driver coughed. Outside, a woman was counting children from the school into the first coach, name by name, getting each name right.

Taavi BrandtThen these are mine. The buses go at three, all of them, nobody comes off. Anyone not on a bus is crew or on a tanker. The wind goes southwest at about twenty to six. I have one number and it does a different job in each hour. Before a torch is lit, thirty-five on the ridge gauge cancels the burn. After it's lit and the strip has gone cold, a gust past thirty-five or one ember across a wall, and nobody lights anything more: every crew comes off the walls and the Marrick track and stands behind the car park wall, and the black and the wet are left to do the work. I don't chase a headfire with a hose. And the woman stays where I can see her. Not in cuffs. At my shoulder.

IlluminaraShe'll want that. She'll want to be told what she's looking at.

Taavi BrandtHale takes her statement afterwards. She isn't pardoned. If this goes wrong at Calder's rate, I'll want it said that I heard her and chose it, not that she tricked me.

Ivet had arrived at the car park door without being noticed, which at eighty-one and with a stick took talent. She stood in the frame and heard the last of it.

Ivet CorrandonI never asked to be pardoned, Chief.

Taavi BrandtNo. I'm told you never asked anyone for anything. That's most of what's wrong with this.

04 Â· The Gully

East wall, then the gully head Â· 16:05

Joss Marren lit thirty metres of the lee at ten past four, with Brandt crouched at his elbow and Ivet in a borrowed folding chair on the track above, her stick across her knees like a sword she had decided not to draw. The flame crept three paces along the grass. It lay down against the stone and went out.

Nobody said anything. A second pass, a little further along: the same. Joss looked at his chief. Brandt put two fingers into the black and held them there.

Taavi BrandtCold.

Ivet CorrandonOf course it's cold. It had nothing to ask for.

Then the smoke from the last small patch did something that Illuminara had been waiting for and did not enjoy being right about. It did not lift. It slid sideways along the ground, thin and grey, leaning north, and went into the mouth of the gully above the Marrick cottages the way water goes into a drain.

IlluminaraChief, look at the smoke. That's my creek. The gully is the old leat, and it is the same shape for air that it is for water. When the wind goes southwest at twenty to six, the headfire won't come at the village over the walls she's combed. It'll come to the one place that has no black behind it, and the leat will take it straight down the cut and out over the top terrace.

Taavi BrandtInto the school and the Marrick cottages.

IlluminaraInto both. And it won't stop at the wall, because no one has burned anything behind that wall for forty years.

Ivet CorrandonEdrin said it burns itself.

IlluminaraWhen did he say it?

Ivet CorrandonThe year after the goats went. Hennie Marrick kept sixty goats on that cut and they ate everything to the clay. Edrin said what he saw. He didn't say the goats had been doing the burning for us.

It was the first time Illuminara had seen the old woman look surprised, and she did not look away from it, out of courtesy. Forty years of a rule that had been right on the day it was spoken.

Taavi BrandtI can't backburn it dry. Not with a headfire two hours out. Dry fuel in a cut that draws like that, I'd be lighting my own chimney.

IlluminaraThen don't burn it dry. The leat has a sluice at the head, and the creek is still running. Forty litres a second for the wet band, down the floor of the cut and over both banks, and a margin either side that your crew combs slow and downhill, Mrs Corrandon's way. A wet strip, a black strip, a wall. The fire has a river on one side and a stone on the other and nowhere to go but out.

Taavi BrandtDownhill. Not up. You said up in your notes.

IlluminaraI did.

Taavi BrandtUp and you're feeding it a ramp. Thirty years I've never lit upslope. Down, a flame has to work for every metre.

IlluminaraDown. Thank you.

She put her finger on his map, on the thin blue cut above the Marrick cottages, and gave him the numbers one at a time so that he could argue with each.

IlluminaraTwo hundred and forty metres from the sluice to the Marrick fence, three terraces, the third at a hundred and eighty. Eleven minutes a hundred metres. Open the sluice at ten to five and the wet front is past the third terrace at ten past. If it isn't, nobody lights the margins, and the crews go to the car park wall. That's the one failure signal, and it comes before any fire does. The wet and the black can run as far as the fence and no further. The two cottages sit forty metres beyond it, past any water I can carry. I would rather say that now than have you find it at six.

Taavi BrandtAnd the wall we just burned?

IlluminaraProves one wall. It's why I trust the margins and not the hill. The hill I'll know when the gully tells us, and I'll tell you what to look for. If I'm right, the headfire enters the head of the cut and slows to a crawl on the wet band. If I'm wrong, it won't slow, and you'll see flame standing on a bank. Either of us can see that from the car park wall.

The wheel at the sluice head had not turned since before Illuminara had been in the district. It took Joss and two of Brandt's volunteers and a length of scaffold pipe for a lever, and she stood by with her watch and counted them through it aloud, as one counts a woman in labour. At ten to five the stone throat filled and the cut began to talk.

At ten past five the wet front shone past the third terrace and the ridge gauge read eighteen. Brandt looked at the one number and then at the other.

Taavi BrandtLight.

Joss went down the east bank with the slow torch and the second crew down the west, walking downhill, a flame having to work for every metre.

05 Â· The Wind Comes Round

Car park wall, below the gully Â· 17:39

The wind turned a minute early.

It came off the west like a door opening in a long house, and the smoke that had leaned north for three hours stood up, thought about it, and leaned the other way. The ridge gauge on Brandt's radio read twenty-nine, then thirty-three. His crews were still in their lines: black behind every wall, a wet band shining down the bottom of the cut, a combed margin either side with the last embers of Joss's slow torch still ticking in it.

Taavi BrandtThirty-four. Nothing left to light, so the other half of my number is the one that counts now. One more and they come off, walking, not asking. Joss, tell them the figure. I want them already turned.

At a quarter to six the gauge read thirty-six.

Taavi BrandtThat's my line. All crews off the walls, off the Marrick track, behind the car park wall. Leave the strip. Go.

They came off in the order he had given them, the wet band and the black walls left standing behind them like a stage with nobody on it, and were over the car park wall with a few minutes in hand. Nobody went up the Marrick track.

The headfire arrived at six minutes to six. It did not come as a wave. It came as a noise, then as a colour on the underside of the smoke, then as a line of brightness across the whole west face of the hill with embers going over it like thrown seed. It reached the first wall. It looked over. Illuminara watched it do exactly what an old woman had said it would do, and find nothing for its mouth.

In the gully it was worse and better. The fire went down into the cut because the cut drew it. Illuminara kept her eyes on the head of the cut and on the two banks, as she had told him she would, and said what she saw aloud so that he could hear it.

IlluminaraSlowing. Banks clear.

The wet band took it to a crawl; it tried both banks and found them combed and black, and no flame stood on either one for longer than it took to be seen. For forty minutes it hissed and leaned and spat at the stone. Then it ran out of road.

Not everything held. The two Marrick cottages lay forty metres beyond the fence where the break ended, exactly where she had marked the edge of her water, and there had been neither time nor crew to carry it further. A gust took an ember across the last wall at ten past six. Joss started along the wall toward the tanker parked at the foot of the Marrick track.

Taavi BrandtNo. They're empty. Let them go.

Joss MarrenChief, they're Hennie'sâ€”

Taavi BrandtThey're stone and slates. Hennie's in the school hall with his dog. This wall is the line, and that track is the wrong side of it. If I send one lad up it and the wind veers again, I've done Calder in a new place. Let them go, Joss.

They went up like paper lanterns, one and then the other, and the whole crew watched them in a silence that Illuminara did not interrupt. Below them the village lay dark and standing. Three hundred roofs. A school with its windows reflecting something that was no longer a threat.

06 Â· What Remained

Corrandon cottage, kitchen Â· 21:40

Constable Hale took the statement at the kitchen table with her hat on her knee and was careful to write only what she was told. Ivet gave it with her bandaged hand flat on the wood. When it was done Hale put away her pen and said nothing about permits, because there was a chief in the doorway and it was his to say.

Taavi BrandtThe Marrick cottages are ash. The test burn was lit without a licence, on my say, against the district's written order. I'll answer for both. I'll answer, too, for the look I gave an old woman at midday.

Ivet CorrandonYou gave me a perfectly good look. I'd have given it to you.

Taavi BrandtMy report starts with your map and ends with my abort line. The review can read it in that order.

Ivet CorrandonThey'll fine me.

Taavi BrandtThey may. I'll stand beside you at the hearing and carry your map in with me, with the gauge log and the times, and say what I saw on that wall and in that cut. That isn't a pardon. I can't give you one. It isn't a promise they'll adopt anything, either.

Ivet CorrandonI don't want a pardon. I want it taught.

Brandt turned his hat once in his hands. It was the nearest thing to hesitation the night had left him.

Taavi BrandtThen a trial, not a promise. Two of your walls first, my crews, in the dry month, my gauge in my hand and my line on the radio. The regional office can quarrel about the paper with a hillside that's still standing.

Ivet said nothing for a while. Edrin's map lay open under the lamp, drawn forty years ago in pencil on the back of a seed catalogue: every wall of Sorrel Gate, each lee shaded.

Ivet CorrandonThen I'll say what I'm giving, since you've said what you are. I don't light alone again. I light with your crew at my elbow, and when you call your signal my torch stops, and I won't need telling twice. And the gully goes on the map, in ink. Edrin said it burns itself. It burned itself because Hennie's goats kept it bare, and when the goats went nobody wrote the date on the rule. I want the date on it.

Illuminara drew one more line on the map at the north, a thin blue cut with an arrow, and wrote beside it, in her small precise hand, leat and wet band, comb downhill; one hill, one wind, one day. She turned the paper toward the old woman.

IlluminaraWould you sign it with his name as well? It's the part he never had time to finish.

Ivet took the pencil in her bandaged hand, and wrote, slowly and without once pressing too hard, Edrin Corrandon, and under it, and Ivet.

What the critic still says doesn't work

An old woman's forbidden firecraft, a chief's fatal memory and Illuminara's water model become a bounded, stoppable defence that saves Sorrel Gate because each of them is willing to find where their own map is wrong.

Tonight's shape

Six scenes across one afternoon and evening: a pattern read as a crime, a kitchen where the pattern is explained, a test the chief can stop, a gully nobody had burned, a wind that turns on time, and a report that begins with an old woman's map.
